Kubo formulas for spin polarization in dissipative relativistic spin hydrodynamics: a first-order gradient expansion approach
Matteo Buzzegoli

Abstract
We use linear response theory to derive both the non-dissipative and dissipative effects of spin polarization for massive and massless interacting spin 1/2 particles in a relativistic fluid. We list and classify all the possible contributions up to first order in gradients of hydrodynamic fields including the axial chemical potential and the spin potential, and we obtain the corresponding Kubo formulas. We find that all the possible dissipative contributions, except those coming from the gradients of spin potential, require a chiral imbalance or parity violating interactions. In a fluid with chiral imbalance we find a chiral version of the spin Hall effect, i.e. a spin polarization is induced by the gradients of temperature and of axial chemical potential in the direction orthogonal to the momentum of the particle and to the gradients. Moreover, we identify several other new non-dissipative contributions that are not present for free fields.



arXiv:
2502.15520

1
Introduction

Spin polarization in a relativistic fluid is an important aspect of the relativistic heavy-ion collision phenomenology where measurements of spin polarization of particles are used to reveal properties of the quark gluon plasma (QGP) formed during the collisions. Based on the analysis of partonic angular momentum transfer
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
and calculations using statistical local thermal equilibrium
[
4
,
5
]
, it was initially realized that thermal vorticity tensor (i.e., the rotation and acceleration fields in the fluid) should induce spin polarization in a fluid. Consequently, for the QGP, spin polarization should be detected in the direction of the large angular momentum generated in non-central collisions. The measurements of the global spin polarization of
\Lambda
and
\bar{\Lambda}

[
6
,
7
,
8
]
, and later of
\Xi
and
\Omega
hyperons
[
9
]
carried out by the STAR collaboration at RHIC and of
\Lambda
and
\bar{\Lambda}
hyperons
[
10
]
by ALICE collaboration at LHC, confirmed the predictions of the vorticity-induced spin polarization based on hydrodynamics and statistical local equilibrium
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
]
.

Subsequent measurements of the spin polarization as a function of momentum
[
17
,
18
,
19
]
, termed “local spin polarization”, differed drastically from the theoretical predictions. In particular, the longitudinal component of the spin polarization vector was found to have an opposite sign compared to the theoretical calculations, giving rise to many theoretical investigations, see for instance the review
[
20
]
. These studies lead to the finding that other hydrodynamic fields in an out-of-equilibrium fluid can also induce spin polarization, such as the thermal shear tensor
[
21
,
22
]
and the gradient of chemical potential
[
23
]
. On the one hand, predictions that include these additional effects
[
24
,
25
,
26
,
27
,
28
,
29
]
can reproduce most of the experimental data. However, a strong sensitivity to transport coefficients and initial hydrodynamic conditions has been observed
[
26
,
29
]
. On the other hand, local spin polarization data raise the question of whether the spin degrees of freedom in the QGP are actually equilibrated
[
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
]
. To address this question it has been necessary to develop spin hydrodynamics, i.e., a framework that includes the evolution of a spin tensor
[
34
,
35
]
.

Evidence of spin alignment of vector mesons (a phenomenon analogous to spin polarization) has also been detected in heavy-ion collisions
[
36
,
37
]
. These observations present a challenging problem because they depend drastically on the collision energy and the type of meson considered. Furthermore, the effect of spin alignment is larger than expected from hydrodynamic models, giving a strong indication that there is a strong spin correlation between the quark and anti-quark that
combine into the vector meson
[
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
,
42
,
43
,
44
,
45
,
46
]
. For recent reviews on spin polarization, spin hydrodynamics and spin alignment we refer to
[
47
,
48
]
.

Therefore, studying all mechanisms that can polarize the spin of a quark in the QGP is central to understanding spin polarization and spin alignment in heavy-ion collisions. Relativistic hydrodynamics has proven to be a very effective tool for studying the properties of the QGP, including spin, because it drastically reduces the effective degrees of freedom from interacting quantum fields to a few classical fields. Taking advantage of the large separation of scales between macroscopic fields and microscopic interactions, hydrodynamics and relativistic statistical mechanics allow for approximating a physical observable with a gradient expansion, which is a series based on the gradients of the hydrodynamic fields
[
49
]
. These terms can be further classified in two categories: the one stemming from the local equilibrium part of the statistical operator, which are not associated to dissipative or dynamical processes and which are proportional to what we call thermal coefficients, and those stemming from the dissipative part of the statistical operator and that are proportional to transport coefficients
[
50
]
. Most of the studies so far focused their attention on the local equilibrium part and obtained the spin polarization of non-interacting particles, and very few attempted to address dissipative contributions
[
51
,
52
,
53
,
54
]
.

The scope of this work is to classify all possible contributions to spin polarization in a dissipative fluid, considering terms up to the first order in the gradient expansion. This work considers a general case including gradients of the fluid velocity (vorticity and shear), vector and axial chemical potentials (the latter describing a fluid with a chiral imbalance, i.e., a difference between right-handed and left-handed particles), and the spin potential (which describes a situation where the spin degrees of freedom have not relaxed to equilibrium). The underlying microscopic theory is not specified; instead, the effects of the hydrodynamic fields will be obtained using linear response theory, and their strength (the thermal and transport coefficients) would have to be calculated using Kubo formulas.
With these formulas, the coefficients are obtained by tracing quantum operators with the statistical operator in the grand canonical equilibrium form.
The symmetries of the equilibrium statistical operator and of the spin polarization vector will be used to classify and discuss the effects obtained with this procedure. It is worth noting that all out-of-equilibrium effects depend on the specific form of the quantum operators used, notably the energy-momentum tensor and the spin tensor. The out-of-equilibrium statistical operator
[
55
]
and, consequently, the spin polarization
[
56
]
, depend on this choice, which is known as the pseudo-gauge choice. We do not specify this choice, because it will not affect the form of the Kubo formulas but only the result of the calculation.

To study all first order contribution to spin polarization, we use the Zubarev non-equilibrium density operator method
[
57
,
58
,
59
,
60
]
, which allows to clearly distinguish the dissipative contributions from the local equilibrium (non-dissipative) ones
[
50
]
. Assuming that the system reaches local thermal equilibrium at some time, the method consist of deriving the covariant form of the statistical operator by maximazing the entropy while constraining the energy-momentum densities and other relevant quantities. It is important to stress that this procedure and the notion of local thermal equilibrium are valid for any underlying quantum field theory. Because of this property and of the premises, the Zubarev method is particularly useful for studying the QGP formed in heavy-ion collisions, especially the quantum effects induced by gradients, including the thermal vorticity. Indeed, this method was used to derive, for the first time, the Kubo formula for the shear viscosity of a quantum relativistic fluid
[
61
]
. Recently, it has been successfully used to study the transport coefficients associated with the gradient expansion in a relativistic fluid
[
62
,
63
,
64
]
, including those associated to the spin potential
[
65
,
66
,
67
,
68
,
69
,
70
,
71
,
72
]
, and to obtain the thermal coefficients related to thermal vorticity
[
73
,
74
,
75
,
76
,
77
]
, to connect the hydrodynamic fields to the spin polarization
[
56
,
78
,
79
,
80
,
53
]
and to the spin alignment
[
81
,
82
,
83
,
53
]
.

The spin polarization of a particle with a definite momentum is obtained from the axial and vector parts of the Wigner function of the corresponding field
[
84
,
85
,
47
]
. The classification of all possible contributions to spin polarization of a spin-½ fermion then boils down to the gradient expansion of the Dirac Wigner function. We perform this expansion up to first order in gradients. Differently from usual physical observable considered in hydrodynamics, like the energy-momentum tensor, the Wigner function is a distribution depending on the point
x
and on momentum
k
. In order to account for the momentum dependence we follow the methods of
[
21
,
23
]
and we decompose the Wigner function in terms construed with the momentum
k
and the hydrodynamic fields. As a result, differently from the usual Kubo formulas, where thermal and transport coefficients depends only on coordinates through equilibrium thermodynamic fields, the thermal and transport coefficients appearing in the Wigner function will also depend on the momentum of the particle state where the spin polarization has been evaluated.

The paper is organized as follows: section
2
reviews the Zubarev method and how to obtain a gradient expansion of a generic local observable. In section
2.2
, the gradient expansion is applied to the spin polarization of a Dirac field, demonstrating that this is obtained from the gradient expansion of the axial and vector parts of the Wigner function. Section
3
provides the general methods for deriving Kubo formulas related to the linear response theory of different types of hydrodynamic fields, classified by their irreducible representation under rotation: scalar, vector, symmetric and antisymmetric tensor and rank-3 spin-like tensor. In section
4
, this method is applied to obtain the linear response of the axial and vector parts of the Wigner function up to the first order in the gradient expansion, to classify the types of coefficients obtained and to determine their Kubo formulas. The main results and their discussion are given in section
5
and we conclude in section
6
.

1.1
Notation

In this paper we use the natural units with
\hbar=c=k_{B}=1
, the Minkowskian metric is diag
(1,-1,-1,-1)
; for the Levi-Civita symbol we use the convention
\epsilon^{0123}=1
. Operators in Hilbert space are denoted by a wide upper hat, e.g.
\widehat{O}
. We will use the relativistic notation with repeated indices assumed to be saturated. To describe hydrodynamic quantities in a relativistic fluid with fluid velocity
u
, where
u^{2}=1
, we define

\Delta^{\mu\nu}=\eta^{\mu\nu}-u^{\mu}u^{\nu},\quad V^{\langle\rho\rangle}=V^{\rho}_{\perp}=\Delta^{\rho}_{\lambda}V^{\lambda},\quad D=u^{\mu}\partial_{\mu},\quad\theta=\partial_{\mu}u^{\mu},

(1)

that are, respectively, the projector to the space orthogonal to
u(x)
, a four-vector orthogonal to
u(x)
, the derivative along the fluid velocity and the rate of expansion. The quantity

\Delta_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\Delta_{\mu\rho}\Delta_{\nu\sigma}+\Delta_{\mu\sigma}\Delta_{\nu\rho}\right)-\frac{1}{3}\Delta_{\mu\nu}\Delta_{\rho\sigma}

(2)

is the projection of a rank-2 tensor to its symmetric traceless part orthogonal to the fluid velocity.
We use the subscripts
S
and
A
in a rank-2 tensor to denote its symmetric and antisymmetric part respectively, for instance

T^{\mu\nu}_{S}=\frac{1}{2}\left(T^{\mu\nu}+T^{\nu\mu}\right),\quad T^{\mu\nu}_{A}=\frac{1}{2}\left(T^{\mu\nu}-T^{\nu\mu}\right).

(3)

The dual of a rank-2 tensor is denoted with a tilde and is defined as follows:

{\widetilde{\varpi}}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\varpi_{\alpha\beta}.

(4)

To describe the Wigner function, that is a distribution function that depends both on coordinates
x
and momentum
k
, we also define the projector

Q^{\mu\nu}=\frac{\Delta^{\mu\nu}}{3}-\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}k_{\perp}^{\nu}}{k_{\perp}^{2}},

(5)

that satisfy the identities

\Delta_{\mu\nu}Q^{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}Q^{\mu\nu}=0,\quad Q_{\mu\nu}Q^{\mu\nu}=\frac{2}{3}.

(6)

The coefficients related to the response of a hydrodynamic field
\mathcal{U}
are classified as follows.
A response coefficient can be thermal (with time-reversal
T
symmetry) or dissipative (breaking time-reversal symmetry), non-chiral or chiral (regarding parity transformation
P
), not charged or charged (regarding charge conjugation
C
). If the coefficient is dissipative, it will be denoted with a bar, if it is chiral it will be written with the fraktur font, and if it is charged it will have a superscript “c”, as illustrated in the following table

\begin{array}[]{|l|cccccccc|}\hline\cr&a_{\mathcal{U}},\,v_{\mathcal{U}}&a_{\mathcal{U}}^{c},\,v_{\mathcal{U}}^{c}&\bar{a}_{\mathcal{U}},\,\bar{v}_{\mathcal{U}}&\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{U}},\,\mathfrak{v}_{\mathcal{U}}&\bar{a}_{\mathcal{U}}^{c},\,\bar{v}_{\mathcal{U}}^{c}&\mathfrak{a}_{\mathcal{U}}^{c},\,\mathfrak{v}_{\mathcal{U}}^{c}&\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathcal{U}},\,\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{\mathcal{U}}&\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{c},\,\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{c}\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{P}&+&+&+&-&+&-&-&-\\
\mathsf{T}&+&+&-&+&-&+&-&-\\
\mathsf{C}&+&-&+&+&-&-&+&-\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(7)

2
Gradient expansion for an out-of-equilibrium fluid

Figure 1:
The foliation of spacetime with
\Sigma(\tau)
a spacelike hypersurface parametrized by
\tau(x)=
const. The normal unit vector to the hypersurfaces is denoted by
n^{\mu}
. The whole region represents the spacetime volume
\Omega
enclosed by two spacelike hypersurface
\Sigma(\tau)
and
\Sigma(\tau+\Delta\tau)
and a timelike boundary
\Theta
.

The description of a relativistic fluid using relativistic quantum statistical mechanics, obtained from the Zubarev method, begins by considering a system that has reached local thermal equilibrium on a 3D spacelike hypersurface
\Sigma(\tau)
at “time”
\tau
, defining a foliation of spacetime (see figure
1
). The system is described by an energy-momentum tensor operator
\widehat{T}^{\mu\nu}
, a spin tensor operator
\widehat{S}^{\lambda,\mu\nu}
, a conserved current
\widehat{j}^{\mu}
and an axial current
\widehat{j}_{A}^{\mu}
obtained from a quantum field theory that we do not specify. At equilibration the system is instead described effectively by the classical tensor fields
T^{\mu\nu}
and
S^{\lambda,\mu\nu}
giving the energy-momentum density and the orbital and spin angular momentum densities, as well as by the currents
j^{\mu}
and
j_{A}^{\mu}
. The statistical operator
\widehat{\rho}
describing the system is then obtained by maximizing the entropy
S=-{\rm tr}\left[\widehat{\rho}\log\widehat{\rho}\right]
with the constraints:

\displaystyle n_{\mu}{\rm tr}\left[\widehat{\rho}\,\widehat{T}^{\mu\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=n_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu},

\displaystyle n_{\lambda}{\rm tr}\left[\widehat{\rho}\,\widehat{S}^{\lambda,\mu\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=n_{\lambda}S^{\lambda,\mu\nu},

(8a)

\displaystyle n_{\mu}{\rm tr}\left[\widehat{\rho}\,\widehat{j}^{\mu}\right]

\displaystyle=n_{\mu}j^{\mu},

\displaystyle n_{\mu}{\rm tr}\left[\widehat{\rho}\,\widehat{j}_{A}^{\mu}\right]

\displaystyle=n_{\mu}j_{A}^{\mu},

(8b)

where
n
is the unit vector orthogonal to
\Sigma(\tau)
. The resulting operator is
[
57
,
58
,
59
,
60
,
74
,
55
]

\widehat{\rho}=\frac{1}{Z}\exp\left\{-\int_{\Sigma(\tau)}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\mu}\left(\widehat{T}^{\mu\nu}\beta_{\nu}-\zeta\,\widehat{j}^{\mu}-\zeta_{A}\,\widehat{j}_{A}^{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda\nu}\widehat{S}^{\mu\lambda\nu}\right)\right\},

(9)

where
\beta
,
\zeta
,
\zeta_{A}
and
\mathfrak{S}
are the relevant Lagrange multiplier functions, and correspond respectively to the four-temperature vector, the ratio between local chemical and axial chemical potentials and temperature and the spin potential
[
55
]
. Since
\beta
is a timelike vector, it is convenient to identify the fluid velocity as

u=\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{\beta^{2}}}=T\,\beta;

(10)

this choice is known as
\beta
or thermodynamic frame
[
59
]
. It is worth noting that, despite the operators being time-dependent, the non-equilibrium statistical operator (
9
) is actually stationary, simply because the time
\tau
at which it is evaluated is fixed. However, it is more convenient to express the statistical operator using a later time 3D hypersurface. For instance, to predict spin polarization in heavy-ion collisions, the statistical operator is evaluated on the freezeout hypersurface
[
47
]
. This can be achieved by applying Gauss’s theorem: the integral in eq. (
9
) over the hypersurface
\Sigma(\tau)
can be expressed as the same integral over
\Sigma(\tau+\Delta\tau)
plus the integral of the divergence of the integrand over the region
\Omega
marked by the two hypersurfaces, provided that the flux of the integrand vanishes at the timelike boundary
\Theta
, as depicted in figure
1
. Therefore, choosing a later time hypersurface
\Sigma

we obtain the statistical operator

\widehat{\rho}=\frac{1}{Z}\exp\Bigg\{-\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\mu}(y)\left(\widehat{T}^{\mu\nu}(y)\beta_{\nu}(y)-\zeta(y)\,\widehat{j}^{\mu}(y)-\zeta_{A}(y)\,\widehat{j}_{A}^{\mu}(y)-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda\nu}(y)\widehat{S}^{\mu\lambda\nu}(y)\right)\\
+\int_{\Omega}{\rm d}\Omega\left[\widehat{T}_{S}^{\mu\nu}\xi_{\mu\nu}+\widehat{T}_{A}^{\mu\nu}\left(\mathfrak{S}_{\mu\nu}-\varpi_{\mu\nu}\right)-\widehat{j}^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\zeta-\nabla_{\mu}\left(\zeta_{A}\widehat{j}_{A}^{\mu}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{S}^{\mu\lambda\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda\nu}\right]\Bigg\},

(11)

where we made use of the equations of motion

\nabla_{\mu}\widehat{T}^{\mu\nu}=0,\quad\nabla_{\lambda}\widehat{S}^{\lambda,\mu\nu}=-2\widehat{T}^{\mu\nu}_{A},\quad\nabla_{\mu}\widehat{j}^{\mu}=0.

(12)

The part expressed as an integral over
\Sigma
is the local equilibrium part and does not generate entropy, while the other part gives rise to entropy production
[
50
]
. For this reason the contributions to physical observables obtained from the latter part are related to dissipative processes, see also
[
60
]
.

Consider now the mean value of an operator
\widehat{O}
at the point
x
(and at momentum
k
when considering the Wigner function), which is
\langle\widehat{O}(x)\rangle={\rm tr}[\,\widehat{\rho}\,\widehat{O}(x)]
. The operator
\widehat{O}
can be a tensor with a set of indices
{(\mu)}
that we omit:
\widehat{O}(x)=\widehat{O}^{(\mu)}(x)
. Taking advantage of the hydrodynamic regime, the mean value can be worked out perturbatively with a gradient expansion of the hydrodynamic fields. For this purpose, the hydrodynamic fields in the local equilibrium part of the statistical operator are expanded in a Taylor series around
x
, that is

\displaystyle\beta_{\nu}(y)=

\displaystyle\beta_{\nu}(x)+(y-x)^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\beta_{\nu}(x)+\cdots

(13a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{\nu}(x)-(y-x)^{\rho}\varpi_{\rho\nu}(x)+(y-x)^{\rho}\xi_{\rho\nu}(x)+\cdots,

\displaystyle\zeta(y)=

\displaystyle\zeta(x)+\partial_{\rho}\zeta(x)(y-x)^{\rho}+\cdots,\quad\zeta_{A}(y)=\zeta_{A}(x)+\partial_{\rho}\zeta_{A}(x)(y-x)^{\rho}+\cdots,

(13b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda\nu}(y)=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda\nu}(x)+(y-x)^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda\nu}(x)+\cdots,

(13c)

and where we defined the thermal vorticity tensor

\varpi_{\rho\sigma}(x)=-\frac{1}{2}\left[\partial_{\rho}\beta_{\sigma}(x)-\partial_{\sigma}\beta_{\rho}(x)\right],

(14)

and the thermal shear tensor

\xi_{\rho\sigma}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\left[\partial_{\rho}\beta_{\sigma}(x)+\partial_{\sigma}\beta_{\rho}(x)\right].

(15)

In this work we keep all the terms that are of first order in derivative, including the derivatives of the axial chemical potential and the spin potential, and we neglect higher derivatives. Even if one expect the gradients of the axial chemical potential and of spin potential to be small, it will be shown that these induce the leading contributions for the dissipative part of the spin polarization in a fluid without parity violating effects and therefore must be included in order to derive the dissipative part of the spin polarization formula. The inclusion of the higher order terms are left for future studies.
Keeping only these terms, the statistical operator (
11
) is approximated as

\widehat{\rho}\simeq\frac{1}{Z}\exp\left\{\widehat{A}+\widehat{B}_{\rm LTE}+\widehat{C}_{\rm D}\right\},\quad\widehat{B}_{\rm LTE}=\widehat{B}_{\varpi}+\widehat{B}_{\xi}+\widehat{B}_{\zeta_{A}}+\widehat{B}_{\mathfrak{S}}+\widehat{B}_{\partial\mathfrak{S}}+\widehat{B}_{\partial\zeta}+\widehat{B}_{\partial\zeta_{A}},

(16)

where the leading term is

\widehat{\rho}_{{\rm eq}(x)}=\frac{{\rm e}^{\widehat{A}}}{{\rm tr}\left[e^{\widehat{A}}\right]}=\frac{{\rm e}^{-\beta(x)\cdot\widehat{P}+\zeta(x)\widehat{Q}}}{{\rm tr}\left[{\rm e}^{-\beta(x)\cdot\widehat{P}+\zeta(x)\widehat{Q}}\right]},\quad\widehat{A}=-\beta(x)\cdot\widehat{P}+\zeta(x)\widehat{Q},

(17)

where
\widehat{P}^{\nu}=\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\mu}\,\widehat{T}^{\mu\nu}
and
\widehat{Q}=\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\mu}\,\widehat{j}^{\mu}
are the four-momentum operator and the total charge, and
\widehat{\rho}_{{\rm eq}(x)}
has the grand canonical form with the hydrodynamic fields evaluated in
x
. The first order local equilibrium corrections are

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{\varpi}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{\rho\sigma}(x)\widehat{J}_{x}^{\rho\sigma},

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{\xi}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\xi_{\rho\sigma}(x)\widehat{\Xi}_{x}^{\rho\sigma},\quad\widehat{B}_{\zeta_{A}}=\zeta_{A}(x)\widehat{Q}_{A},

(18a)

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{\mathfrak{S}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{\rho\sigma}(x)-\varpi_{\rho\sigma}(x))\widehat{S}^{\rho\sigma},

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{\partial\mathfrak{S}}=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda\nu}(x)\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\rho}(y)\,(y-x)^{\mu}\widehat{S}^{\rho,\lambda\nu}(y),

(18b)

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{\partial\zeta}=

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}\zeta(x)\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\lambda}(y)\,(y-x)^{\rho}\widehat{j}^{\lambda}(y),

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{\partial\zeta_{A}}=

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}\zeta_{A}(x)\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\lambda}(y)\,(y-x)^{\rho}\widehat{j}_{A}^{\lambda}(y),

(18c)

with the angular-boost momentum operator around
x
given by

\widehat{J}_{x}^{\rho\sigma}=\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\lambda}(y)\,\widehat{\mathcal{J}}^{\lambda,\rho\sigma}=\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\lambda}(y)\left[(y-x)^{\rho}\widehat{T}^{\lambda\sigma}(y)-(y-x)^{\sigma}\widehat{T}^{\lambda\rho}(y)+\widehat{S}^{\lambda,\rho\sigma}(y)\right],

(19a)

and the other global operators are defined by

\displaystyle\widehat{\Xi}_{x}^{\rho\sigma}(\Sigma)=

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\lambda}(y)\left[(y-x)^{\rho}\widehat{T}^{\lambda\sigma}(y)+(y-x)^{\sigma}\widehat{T}^{\lambda\rho}(y)\right],

(19b)

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{A}(\Sigma)=

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\lambda}(y)\,\widehat{j}_{A}^{\lambda}(y),\quad\widehat{S}^{\rho\sigma}(\Sigma)=\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\lambda}(y)\,\widehat{S}^{\lambda,\rho\sigma}(y).

(19c)

Since
\nabla_{\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{J}}^{\lambda,\rho\sigma}=0
, the operator
\widehat{J}
is independent of
\Sigma
, whereas the other operators are not conserved and depend on the hypersurface
\Sigma
. The dissipative part is included in

\widehat{C}_{\rm D}=\int_{\Omega}{\rm d}\Omega\left[\widehat{T}_{S}^{\mu\nu}\xi_{\mu\nu}+\widehat{T}_{A}^{\mu\nu}\left(\mathfrak{S}_{\mu\nu}-\varpi_{\mu\nu}\right)-\widehat{j}^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\zeta-\widehat{j}_{A}^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\zeta_{A}-\zeta_{A}\nabla_{\mu}\widehat{j}_{A}^{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{S}^{\mu\lambda\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda\nu}\right],

(20)

which we later expand in gradients.
Because the system is assumed to be in a hydrodynamic regime with small correlation lengths, the operator
\widehat{A}
is dominant compared to all the other operators that are collected in
\widehat{B}=\widehat{B}_{\rm LTE}+\widehat{C}_{\rm D}
. Consequently, these corrections can be accounted for with linear response theory.

Taking advantage of the Kubo identity

{\rm e}^{\widehat{A}+\widehat{B}}\simeq{\rm e}^{\widehat{A}}+\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}x\,{\rm e}^{-z\widehat{A}}\,\widehat{B}\,{\rm e}^{z\widehat{A}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\widehat{B}^{2}\right),

(21)

and including the partition function
Z={\rm tr}[\widehat{A}+\widehat{B}]
, the first-order expansion of the expectation value of an operator
\widehat{O}(x)
, see for instance for this formalism
[
73
,
74
]
, is

\langle\widehat{O}(x)\rangle={\rm tr}\left[\,\widehat{\rho}\,\widehat{O}(x)\right]\simeq\langle\widehat{O}(x)\rangle_{\beta}+\langle\widehat{B}_{[1]}\,\widehat{O}(x)\rangle_{\beta,c}+\cdots

(22)

where the subscript “c” denotes the connected correlator

\langle\widehat{B}\,\widehat{O}\rangle_{c}=\langle\widehat{B}\,\widehat{O}\rangle-\langle\widehat{B}\rangle\langle\widehat{O}\rangle,

(23)

and the subscript
\beta
denotes thermal averages with the leading order statistical operator, that is

\langle\widehat{O}(x)\rangle_{\beta}={\rm tr}\left[\widehat{\rho}_{{\rm eq}(x)}\widehat{O}(x)\right]=\frac{{\rm tr}\left[{\rm e}^{\widehat{A}}\widehat{O}(x)\right]}{{\rm tr}\left[{\rm e}^{\widehat{A}}\right]}=\frac{{\rm tr}\left[{\rm e}^{-\beta(x)\cdot\widehat{P}+\zeta(x)\widehat{Q}}\widehat{O}(x)\right]}{{\rm tr}\left[{\rm e}^{-\beta(x)\cdot\widehat{P}+\zeta(x)\widehat{Q}}\right]},

(24)

and we defined

\widehat{B}_{[1]}=\int_{0}^{1}\mskip-3.0mu{\rm d}z\,\widehat{B}_{[z]}=\int_{0}^{1}\mskip-3.0mu{\rm d}z\,{\rm e}^{-z\widehat{A}}\widehat{B}{\rm e}^{z\widehat{A}},

(25)

with
\widehat{B}_{[z]}\equiv{\rm e}^{-z\widehat{A}}\widehat{B}{\rm e}^{z\widehat{A}}
. Note that the operators
\widehat{B}_{\rm LTE}
and
\widehat{C}_{\rm D}
in the statistical operator (
16
) moved from the exponent to linear terms; the total contribution is simply obtained by summing all the terms from each operator.

The different contributions contained in
\widehat{B}=\widehat{B}_{\rm LTE}+\widehat{C}_{\rm D}
will now be analyzed. In general,
\mathcal{U}
denotes a hydrodynamic field or its derivative, that is

\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}=\varpi_{\rho\sigma},\,\xi_{\rho\sigma},\,\partial_{\rho}\zeta,\,\zeta_{A},\,\partial_{\rho}\zeta_{A},\,\mathfrak{S}_{\rho\sigma},\,\partial_{\tau}\mathfrak{S}_{\rho\sigma},

(26)

where
(\alpha)
represents a set of indices.
First, consider the dissipative part. Using this notation, each term in
\widehat{C}_{\rm D}
can be written as

\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{U}}=c_{\mathcal{U}}\int_{\Omega}{\rm d}\Omega\,\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}(x_{2})\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{(\alpha)}(x_{2}),

(27)

with
c_{\mathcal{U}}
a number.
The dissipative contribution of
\mathcal{U}
to the mean value
O
is then

\Delta_{\mathcal{U},\,{\rm D}}O(x)=\frac{{\rm tr}\left[{\rm e}^{\widehat{A}+\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{U}}}\widehat{O}(x)\right]}{{\rm tr}\left[{\rm e}^{\widehat{A}+\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{U}}}\right]}-\langle\widehat{O}(x)\rangle_{\beta}=\langle\widehat{C}_{\mathcal{U}\,[1]}\,\widehat{O}(x)\rangle_{\beta,c}.

(28)

The correlator on the RHS of (
28
) can be worked out and written as

\Delta_{\mathcal{U},\,{\rm D}}O(x)=c_{\mathcal{U}}\frac{{\rm i}}{|\beta(x)|}\int_{-\infty}^{t}{\rm d}^{4}x_{2}\int_{-\infty}^{t_{2}}{\rm d}s\,\left\langle\left[\widehat{O}(x),\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{(\alpha)}(s,\,\bm{x}_{2})\right]\right\rangle_{\beta(x)}\,\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}(x_{2})\,,

(29)

where
|\beta(x)|=\sqrt{\beta(x)^{2}}=T(x)^{-1}
. The derivation of eq. (
29
) from eq. (
28
) is a known result
[
61
,
62
,
63
,
50
,
64
]
and will not be repeated here. However, the important properties and approximations used are listed:

•

The integral over the volume
\Omega
between the initial time
\tau
and the time at the point
x
has been approximated as the volume within the two hyperplanes with normal
u^{\mu}
at the point
x
and at the initial time hypersurface
\tau
.
1
1

1

In heavy-ion collisions, where the freezeout hypersurface is usually chosen as the final time hypersurface, it is questionable whether this remains a good approximation. However, similarly to what is done in appendix
A
for the local equilibrium corrections, expressing the linear response correction with the retarded Green’s function allows the volume integral to be factored into
\int_{\Omega}{\rm d}^{4}x_{2}{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}q(y-x)}
that can be modified accordingly.

•

The operators
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{(\alpha)}(x_{2})
commute with
\widehat{Q}
, such that

{\rm e}^{z\left(\beta(x)\cdot\widehat{P}-\zeta(x)\widehat{Q}\right)}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{(\alpha)}(x_{2}){\rm e}^{-z\left(\beta(x)\cdot\widehat{P}-\zeta(x)\widehat{Q}\right)}=\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{(\alpha)}\left(x_{2}-{\rm i}z\sqrt{\beta(x)^{2}}\right).

(30)

•

The correlation between the operator
\widehat{O}
at time
t
and
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{(\alpha)}
at the (infinitely) remote past is vanishing:

\begin{split}\lim_{t_{0}\to\infty}\langle\widehat{O}(x)\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{(\alpha)}(t_{0},\bm{x}_{2})\rangle_{\beta(x)}\simeq&\lim_{t_{0}\to\infty}\langle\widehat{O}(x)\rangle_{\beta(x)}\langle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{(\alpha)}(t_{0},\bm{x}_{2})\rangle_{\beta(x)}\\
=&\langle\widehat{O}(x)\rangle_{\beta(x)}\langle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{(\alpha)}(x_{2})\rangle_{\beta(x)}\,.\end{split}

(31)

From eq. (
29
) we see that in principle the dissipative effects on the observable
\widehat{O}
depends on the whole history of the hydrodynamic fields
\mathcal{U}
. However, in the hydrodynamic regime considered in this work, the correlation length between
\widehat{O}(x)
and
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{U}}(x_{2})
is typically much smaller than the distance over which the gradients of
\mathcal{U}
have significant variation. Therefore, in the hydrodynamic limit, we obtain a good approximation by replacing the hydrodynamic field
\mathcal{U}(x_{2})
with its Taylor expansion around the point
x
where we are evaluating our observable.
The dissipative part
\widehat{C}_{\rm D}
is then decomposed in

\widehat{C}_{\rm D}\simeq\widehat{C}_{\xi}+\widehat{C}_{\partial\zeta}+\widehat{C}_{\zeta_{A}}+\widehat{C}_{\partial\zeta_{A}}+\widehat{C}_{\mathfrak{S}}+\widehat{C}_{\partial\mathfrak{S}},

(32)

where

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\xi}=

\displaystyle\xi_{\rho\sigma}(x)\int{\rm d}^{4}x\,\widehat{T}^{\rho\sigma}_{S}(x_{2}),\quad\widehat{C}_{\partial\zeta}=-\partial_{\rho}\zeta(x)\int{\rm d}^{4}x\,\widehat{j}^{\rho}(x_{2}),

(33a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\zeta_{A}}=

\displaystyle-\zeta_{A}(x)\int{\rm d}^{4}x\,\partial_{\lambda}\widehat{j}_{A}^{\lambda}(x_{2}),\,\widehat{C}_{\partial\zeta_{A}}=-\partial_{\rho}\zeta_{A}(x)\int{\rm d}^{4}x\,\left[\widehat{j}_{A}^{\rho}(x_{2})+(x_{2}-x)^{\rho}\partial_{\lambda}\widehat{j}^{\lambda}_{A}\right],

(33b)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\mathfrak{S}}=

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{S}_{\rho\sigma}(x)-\varpi_{\rho\sigma}(x))\int{\rm d}^{4}x\,\widehat{T}^{\rho\sigma}_{A}(x_{2}),

(33c)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\partial\mathfrak{S}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\tau}\mathfrak{S}_{\rho\sigma}(x)\int{\rm d}^{4}x\left[\widehat{S}^{\tau,\rho\sigma}(x_{2})-2(x_{2}-x)^{\tau}\widehat{T}^{\rho\sigma}_{A}(x_{2})\right].

(33d)

The linear response theory of these terms result in

\Delta_{\mathcal{U},\,{\rm D}}O(x)=\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}(x)\,c_{\mathcal{U}}\,\left(\widehat{O},\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{(\alpha)}\right)_{\rm D}\,,

(34)

where we defined

\boxed{\left(\widehat{X},\,\widehat{Y}\right)_{\rm D}=\frac{{\rm i}}{|\beta(x)|}\int_{-\infty}^{t}{\rm d}^{4}x_{2}\int_{-\infty}^{t_{2}}{\rm d}s\,\left\langle\left[\widehat{X}(x),\,\widehat{Y}(s,\,\bm{x}_{2})\right]\right\rangle_{\beta(x)}.}

(35)

All the transport coefficients will be expressed with this thermal correlator. More commonly Kubo formulas are expressed in momentum space. Indeed, the correlator (
35
) can be expressed with the retarded Green’s function in momentum space defined by

\displaystyle G^{R}_{\widehat{X}\widehat{Y}}(x-x_{2})=

\displaystyle-{\rm i}\theta(x-x_{2})\left\langle\left[\widehat{X}(x),\,\widehat{Y}(x_{2})\right]\right\rangle_{\beta(x)},

(36)

\displaystyle G^{R}_{\widehat{X}\widehat{Y}}(x)=

\displaystyle\int\frac{{\rm d}^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}p\cdot x}G^{R}_{\widehat{X}\widehat{Y}}(p).

(37)

Working out the eq. (
35
), see
[
61
,
62
,
63
,
50
,
64
]
and appendix
A
, we eventually obtain

\left(\widehat{X},\,\widehat{Y}\right)_{\rm D}=-\frac{1}{|\beta(x)|}u^{\lambda}\lim_{p\cdot u\to 0}\lim_{p_{\perp}\to 0}\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\lambda}}{\rm Im}\,G^{R}_{\widehat{X}\widehat{Y}}(p).

(38)

This exact method and these assumptions were used to obtained for the first time the Kubo formula for the shear viscosity in a relativistic fluid with an underlying quantum field theory as microscopic interactions
[
61
]
. In the first order approximation adopted here (where past correlation of the hydrodynamic field and microscopic interactions are neglected) we obtained the linear response as a static (zero-frequency) retarded Green’s function. The study of higher orders can be understood as including non-local (memory) effects and it leads to consider retarded Green’s functions at finite frequency, or at least its higher derivative
[
64
]
. The study of these higher corrections in the derivative expansion naturally generates finite relaxation time scales in the transport equations, see for instance
[
86
,
87
,
88
,
64
]
. We conclude the discussion of the dissipative part by considering the second terms inside the integral of
\widehat{C}_{\partial\zeta_{A}}
and in
\widehat{C}_{\partial\mathfrak{S}}
of eqs. (
33b
) and (
33d
). In these cases it could be convenient to split the coordinate from the operator, obtaining (see appendix
A
)

\left(\widehat{O},\,\int{\rm d}^{4}x_{2}(x_{2}-x)^{\rho}\,\partial_{\lambda}\widehat{j}^{\lambda}_{A}\right)_{\rm D}=-\frac{1}{|\beta(x)|}u^{\sigma}\lim_{p\cdot u\to 0}\lim_{p_{\perp}\to 0}\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\sigma}}\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\rho}}{\rm Re}\,G^{R}_{\widehat{O}\partial_{\lambda}\widehat{j}^{\lambda}_{A}}(p),

(39)

and

\left(\widehat{O},\,\int{\rm d}^{4}x_{2}(x_{2}-x)^{\tau}\,\widehat{T}^{\rho\sigma}_{A}\right)_{\rm D}=-\frac{1}{|\beta(x)|}u^{\sigma}\lim_{p\cdot u\to 0}\lim_{p_{\perp}\to 0}\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\sigma}}\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\tau}}{\rm Re}\,G^{R}_{\widehat{O}\widehat{T}^{\rho\sigma}_{A}}(p).

(40)

For the non-dissipative part, each term in the operator
\widehat{B}_{\rm LTE}
has the form

\widehat{B}_{\mathcal{U}}=b_{\mathcal{U}}\,\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}(x)\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{(\alpha)},

(41)

resulting in the following contribution to the mean value of the operator
\widehat{O}
:

\Delta_{\mathcal{U},\,{\rm LTE}}O(x)=\frac{{\rm tr}\left[{\rm e}^{\widehat{A}+\widehat{B}_{\mathcal{U}}}\widehat{O}(x)\right]}{{\rm tr}\left[{\rm e}^{\widehat{A}+\widehat{B}_{\mathcal{U}}}\right]}-\langle\widehat{O}(x)\rangle_{\beta}=\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}(x)\,b_{\mathcal{U}}\,\left(\widehat{O},\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{(\alpha)}\right)_{\rm LTE}

(42)

where

\boxed{\left(\widehat{X},\,\widehat{Y}\right)_{\rm LTE}=\int_{0}^{|\beta|}\frac{{\rm d}\tau}{|\beta(x)|}\langle\widehat{Y}_{[\tau/|\beta|]}\widehat{X}(x)\rangle_{\beta(x),\,{\rm c}}\,,}

(43)

and we remind

\widehat{Y}_{[\tau/|\beta|]}={\rm e}^{\frac{\tau}{|\beta|}\left(\beta(x)\cdot\widehat{P}-\zeta(x)\widehat{Q}\right)}\widehat{Y}{\rm e}^{-\frac{\tau}{|\beta|}\left(\beta(x)\cdot\widehat{P}-\zeta(x)\widehat{Q}\right)}.

(44)

To write the correlator (
43
) in terms of retarded Green’s function in momentum space it is convenient to consider three different cases. The first is the response to thermal vorticity
\widehat{B}_{\varpi}
which is given in terms of the angular-boost generators
\widehat{J}
that are conserved operators and are present even at global thermodynamic equilibrium. In this case we have:

\widehat{B}_{\varpi}=\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{\rho\sigma}(x)\widehat{J}_{x}^{\rho\sigma},\quad\Delta_{\varpi,\,{\rm LTE}}O(x)=\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{\rho\sigma}(x)\,\left(\widehat{O},\,\widehat{J}_{x}^{\rho\sigma}\right)_{\rm LTE},

(45)

where

\begin{split}\left(\widehat{O},\,\widehat{J}_{x}^{\rho\sigma}\right)_{\rm LTE}=&\int_{0}^{|\beta|}\frac{{\rm d}\tau}{|\beta(x)|}\langle\widehat{J}^{\rho\sigma}_{x[\tau/|\beta|]}\widehat{O}(x)\rangle_{\beta(x),\,{\rm c}}\\
=&\frac{2{\rm i}}{|\beta(x)|}\int_{-\infty}^{t}{\rm d}t_{2}\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\lambda}(y)(y-x)^{\rho}\left\langle\left[\widehat{O}(x),\,\widehat{T}_{B}^{\lambda\sigma}(t_{2},\,\bm{y}_{2})\right]\right\rangle_{\beta(x)}.\end{split}

(46)

The operator
\widehat{J}
is a conserved quantity and it is independent of the pseudo-gauge used to compute it; therefore it is more convenient to use the symmetric Belinfante energy-momentum tensor
\widehat{T}_{B}
as done above, such that we do not have to include an explicit spin part. The factor
2
already consider that the correlator must be contracted with the antisymmetric tensor
\varpi
. Being a conserved operator,
\widehat{J}
is also independent of the hypersurface and without loss of generality, we can choose
\Sigma
as the hyperplane with normal
u(x)
, obtaining

\begin{split}\left(\widehat{O},\,\widehat{J}_{x}^{\rho\sigma}\right)_{\rm LTE}=&\frac{2{\rm i}}{|\beta(x)|}u_{\lambda}(x)\int_{-\infty}^{t}{\rm d}t_{2}\int{\rm d}^{3}y\,(y-x)^{\rho}\left\langle\left[\widehat{O}(x),\,\widehat{T}_{B}^{\lambda\sigma}(t_{2},\,\bm{y}_{2})\right]\right\rangle_{\beta(x)}\\
=&\frac{2{\rm i}}{|\beta(x)|}u_{\lambda}(x)\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\rm d}t_{2}\int{\rm d}^{3}x_{2}\,x_{2}^{\rho}\left\langle\left[\widehat{O}(0),\,\widehat{T}_{B}^{\lambda\sigma}(t_{2},\,\bm{x}_{2})\right]\right\rangle_{\beta(x)},\end{split}

(47)

which can be written as (see appendix
A
)

\left(\widehat{O},\,\widehat{J}_{x}^{\rho\sigma}\right)_{\rm LTE}=\frac{1}{|\beta(x)|}u_{\lambda}(x)\lim_{u\cdot p\to 0}\lim_{p_{\perp}\to 0}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{\rho}}{\rm Im}\,G^{R}_{\widehat{T}_{B}^{\lambda\sigma}\widehat{O}}(p).

(48)

All the other terms resulting from the linear response of the local thermal equilibrium operators
\widehat{B}_{\rm LTE}
depend on the specific form of the hypersurface
\Sigma
. This is a consequence of being out-of-equilibrium contributions, yet non-dissipative, and consequently of being derived from non conserved quantum operators. Consider first
\widehat{B}_{\zeta_{A}}
and
\widehat{B}_{\mathfrak{S}}
; they can be written as

\widehat{B}_{\mathcal{U},0}=b_{\mathcal{U}}\,\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}(x)\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{U},0}^{(\alpha)}=b_{\mathcal{U}}\,\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}(x)\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\lambda}(y)\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\lambda(\alpha)}(y).

(49)

Their linear contribution to the mean value of an observable is

\Delta_{\mathcal{U},0,\,{\rm LTE}}O(x)=\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}(x)\,b_{\mathcal{U}}\,\left(\widehat{O},\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{U},0}^{(\alpha)}\right)_{\rm LTE}.

(50)

If the hypersurface has the same form as the freezeout in heavy-ion collisions, the correlator can be written as (see appendix
A
)

\left(\widehat{O},\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{U},0}^{(\alpha)}\right)_{\rm LTE}=\frac{\hat{t}_{\lambda}}{|\beta(x)|}\lim_{p\cdot u\to 0}\lim_{p_{T}\to 0}{\rm Re}\,G^{R}_{\widehat{O}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\lambda(\alpha)}_{\mathcal{U}}}(p),

(51)

with
\widehat{t}
is the time direction in the center of mass frame; instead, approximating the hypersurface with an hyperplane orthogonal to
u
(see appendix
A
):

\begin{split}\left(\widehat{O},\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{U},0}^{(\alpha)}\right)_{\rm LTE}\simeq&\frac{u_{\lambda}}{|\beta(x)|}\lim_{p\cdot u\to 0}\lim_{p_{T}\to 0}{\rm Re}\,G^{R}_{\widehat{O}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\lambda(\alpha)}_{\mathcal{U}}}(p).\end{split}

(52)

Lastly, the operators
\widehat{B}_{\xi},\,\widehat{B}_{\partial\mathfrak{S}},\,\widehat{B}_{\partial\zeta},\,\widehat{B}_{\partial\zeta_{A}}
all have the form

\widehat{B}_{\mathcal{U},1}=b_{\mathcal{U}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\rho(\alpha_{2})}(x)\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{U},1}^{\rho(\alpha_{2})}=b_{\mathcal{U}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\rho(\alpha_{2})}(x)\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma_{\lambda}(y)\,(y-x)^{\rho}\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{U}}^{\lambda(\alpha_{2})}(y),

(53)

and their contribution is expressed as

\Delta_{\mathcal{U},1,\,{\rm LTE}}O(x)=\mathcal{U}_{\rho(\alpha_{2})}(x)\,b_{\mathcal{U}}\,\left(\widehat{O},\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{U},1}^{\rho(\alpha_{2})}\right)_{\rm LTE}.

(54)

In momentum space, we can show that (see appendix
A
) the correlator becomes

\left(\widehat{O},\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{U},1}^{\rho(\alpha_{2})}\right)_{\rm LTE}=\frac{\hat{t}_{\lambda}}{|\beta(x)|}\lim_{p\cdot u\to 0}\lim_{p_{T}\to 0}\Delta^{\rho}_{\sigma}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{\sigma}}{\rm Im}\,G^{R}_{\widehat{O}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\lambda(\alpha_{2})}_{\mathcal{U}}}(p)

(55)

when
\Sigma
is the freezeout hypersurface, or, approximating the hypersurface with an hyperplane orthogonal to
u
(see appendix
A
):

\begin{split}\left(\widehat{O},\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{U},1}^{\rho(\alpha_{2})}\right)_{\rm LTE}\simeq&\frac{u_{\lambda}}{|\beta(x)|}\lim_{p\cdot u\to 0}\lim_{p_{T}\to 0}\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{\rho}}{\rm Im}\,G^{R}_{\widehat{O}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\lambda(\alpha_{2})}_{\mathcal{U}}}(p).\end{split}

(56)

2.1
Chiral coefficients

Many dissipative corrections to the spin polarization are given by chiral transport coefficients, meaning that they are obtained with thermal correlators of two operators that transform with opposite sign under a parity transformation
\widehat{\mathsf{P}}
; that is, if

\widehat{\mathsf{P}}\,\widehat{O}\,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}=\eta_{O}\widehat{O},\quad\widehat{\mathsf{P}}\,\widehat{B}\,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}=\eta_{B}\widehat{B},

(57)

the coefficient given by
\langle\widehat{O}\,\widehat{B}\rangle_{\beta}
is chiral if
\eta_{O}\,\eta_{B}=-1
.
Notable examples of chiral coefficients are those related to the chiral magnetic effect and to the chiral vortical effect. Chiral coefficients are not vanishing only if the system has interactions that breaks parity or if there is a chiral imbalance
\zeta_{A}
. Because otherwise
\widehat{\mathsf{P}}\,\widehat{\rho}_{{\rm eq}(x)}\,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}=\widehat{\rho}_{{\rm eq}(x)}
and we have

\begin{split}\langle\widehat{O}\,\widehat{B}\rangle_{\beta}=&{\rm tr}\left[\widehat{\rho}_{{\rm eq}(x)}\,\widehat{O}\,\widehat{B}\right]={\rm tr}\left[\widehat{\rho}_{{\rm eq}(x)}\,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}\,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}\,\widehat{O}\,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}\,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}\,\widehat{B}\,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}\,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}\right]\\
=&\eta_{O}\,\eta_{B}{\rm tr}\left[\widehat{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}\widehat{\rho}_{{\rm eq}(x)}\,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}\widehat{O}\,\widehat{B}\right]=-\langle\widehat{O}\,\widehat{B}\rangle_{\beta}=0.\end{split}

(58)

In the presence of a chiral imbalance, we can obtain a chiral coefficient including the axial chemical potential in the equilibrium statistical operator

\widehat{\rho}_{{\rm eq}(x)}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{\rm eq}}\exp\left\{-\beta(x)\cdot\widehat{P}+\zeta(x)\widehat{Q}+\zeta_{A}(x)\widehat{Q}_{A}\right\}

(59)

as done in
[
74
]
for free chiral fermions. Another option to estimate them is to include a higher-order correction in
\zeta_{A}
.
This is done by including higher order of
\zeta_{A}(x)\widehat{Q}_{A}
in the linear response expansion. Consider for instance a chiral coefficient resulting from an operator
\widehat{B}_{X}
; we expand as before the statistical operator as follows

\widehat{\rho}\simeq\frac{1}{Z}\exp\left\{\widehat{A}+\zeta_{A}(x)\widehat{Q}_{A}+\widehat{B}_{X}+\dots\right\}

(60)

and the average of an operator is obtained from the linear response theory at second order in the axial charge:

\langle\widehat{O}(x)\rangle={\rm tr}\left[\,\widehat{\rho}\,\widehat{O}(x)\right]\simeq\langle\widehat{O}(x)\rangle_{\beta}+\langle\widehat{B}_{[1]}\,\widehat{O}(x)\rangle_{\beta,c}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\widehat{B}_{[2]}\,\widehat{O}(x)\rangle_{\beta,c}+\cdots

(61)

where

\widehat{B}_{[2]}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\lambda_{1}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\lambda_{2}T_{\lambda}\left\{\widehat{B}_{[\lambda_{1}]}\widehat{B}_{[\lambda_{2}]}\right\}

(62)

where
T_{\lambda}
is the time-ordering operator with respect to the variables
\lambda_{1}

and
\lambda_{2}
, and it is straightforward to see that

\frac{1}{2}\langle\widehat{B}_{[2]}\widehat{O}(x)\rangle_{\beta,{\rm c}}=\left(\widehat{B},\,\widehat{B},\,\widehat{O}(x)\right),

(63)

where we defined the three-point correlation function as

\begin{split}\big(\widehat{Y},\,\widehat{Z},\,\widehat{X}\big)\equiv&\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\lambda_{1}\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}\lambda_{2}T_{\lambda}\left\{\langle\widehat{Y}_{[\lambda_{1}]}\widehat{Z}_{[\lambda_{2}]}\widehat{X}\rangle_{\beta}\right.-\langle\widehat{Y}_{[\lambda_{1}]}\widehat{Z}_{[\lambda_{2}]}\rangle_{\beta}\langle\widehat{X}\rangle_{\beta}-\langle\widehat{Y}_{[\lambda_{1}]}\rangle_{\beta}\langle\widehat{Z}_{[\lambda_{2}]}\widehat{X}\rangle_{\beta}\\
&\left.-\langle\widehat{Z}_{[\lambda_{2}]}\rangle_{\beta}\langle\widehat{Y}_{[\lambda_{1}]}\widehat{X}\rangle_{\beta}+2\langle\widehat{Y}_{[\lambda_{1}]}\rangle_{\beta}\langle\widehat{Z}_{[\lambda_{2}]}\rangle_{\beta}\langle\widehat{X}\rangle_{\beta}\right\}.\end{split}

(64)

It is also straightforward to find the identities:

\begin{split}\left(\widehat{A}+\widehat{B},\,\widehat{C},\,\widehat{O}\right)=&\left(\widehat{A},\,\widehat{C},\,\widehat{O}\right)+\left(\widehat{B},\,\widehat{C},\,\widehat{O}\right)\\
\left(\widehat{A},\,\widehat{B},\,\widehat{O}\right)=&\left(\widehat{B},\,\widehat{A},\,\widehat{O}\right).\end{split}

(65)

The order
\mathcal{O}(\delta_{A}\partial^{1})
is then only contained in:

\left(\zeta_{A}(x)\widehat{Q}_{A}+\widehat{B}_{X},\,\zeta_{A}(x)\widehat{Q}_{A}+\widehat{B}_{X},\,\widehat{O}(x)\right)=\zeta_{A}(x)^{2}\left(\widehat{Q}_{A},\,\widehat{Q}_{A},\,\widehat{O}(x)\right)\\
+2\zeta_{A}(x)\left(\widehat{Q}_{A},\,\widehat{B}_{X},\,\widehat{O}(x)\right)+\left(\widehat{B}_{X},\,\widehat{B}_{X},\,\widehat{O}(x)\right)\simeq 2\zeta_{A}(x)\left(\widehat{Q}_{A},\,\widehat{B}_{X},\,\widehat{O}(x)\right).

In conclusion, a chiral coefficient obtained at first order in
\widehat{B}_{X}
is approximated as:

\left(\widehat{B}_{X},\,\widehat{O}(x)\right)\simeq 2\zeta_{A}(x)\left(\widehat{Q}_{A},\,\widehat{B}_{X},\,\widehat{O}(x)\right).

(66)

2.2
Spin polarization of fermions in a dissipative fluid

The main purpose of this work is to determine the spin polarization of a Dirac field in a fluid, considering terms up to the first order in the derivative expansion. To that aim, the covariant Wigner operator of a Dirac field must be introduced. The Wigner operator is defined as the Wigner transform of the two-point function

\widehat{W}(x,\,k)_{AB}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mskip-3.0mu\int\mskip-3.0mu{\rm d}^{4}y\,{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}k\cdot y}:\widehat{\bar{\Psi}}_{B}\left(x+\frac{y}{2}\right)\widehat{\Psi}_{A}\left(x-\frac{y}{2}\right):\,,

(67)

where
\widehat{\Psi}
is the Dirac field, and
:\,:
denotes the normal ordering of creation and destruction operators. As being constructed with the spinors
\widehat{\Psi}
, the Wigner operator is a
4\times 4
spinoral matrix. With the Wigner transform the non-local two-point function has become an operator depending on the coordinate
x
and on the off-shell momentum
k
. We instead refer to the mean value of the Wigner operator as the Wigner function:

W(x,\,k)={\rm tr}\left[\widehat{\rho}\,\widehat{W}(x,\,k)\right]=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mskip-3.0mu\int\mskip-3.0mu{\rm d}^{4}y\,{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}k\cdot y}\left\langle:\widehat{\bar{\Psi}}_{B}\left(x+\frac{y}{2}\right)\widehat{\Psi}_{A}\left(x-\frac{y}{2}\right):\right\rangle.

(68)

Without loss of generality, the Wigner function can be expanded on the basis of the 16 independent generators of Clifford algebra as

W(x,\,k)=\frac{1}{4}\left[\mathcal{F}(x,\,k)+{\rm i}\gamma^{5}\mathcal{P}(x,\,k)+\gamma^{\mu}\mathcal{V}_{\mu}(x,\,k)+\gamma^{5}\gamma^{\mu}\mathcal{A}_{\mu}(x,\,k)+\frac{{\rm i}}{4}[\gamma^{\mu},\,\gamma^{\nu}]\mathcal{S}_{\mu\nu}(x,\,k)\right].

(69)

The component functions can be extracted from the Wigner function by tracing over spinoral indices, for example:

\mathcal{F}(x,\,k)={\rm tr}_{4}\left[W(x,\,k)\right],\quad\mathcal{V}^{\mu}(x,\,k)={\rm tr}_{4}\left[\gamma^{\mu}\,W(x,\,k)\right],\quad\mathcal{A}^{\mu}(x,\,k)={\rm tr}_{4}\left[\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{5}\,W(x,\,k)\right].

(70)

The Wigner operator can be further decomposed into three different terms that correspond to the particle (future timelike), antiparticle (past timelike), and spacelike parts:

\begin{split}\widehat{W}(x,\,k)=&\widehat{W}(x,\,k)\theta(k^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)+\widehat{W}(x,\,k)\theta(k^{2})\theta(-k\cdot u)+\widehat{W}(x,\,k)\theta(-k^{2})\\
=&\widehat{W}_{+}(x,\,k)+\widehat{W}_{-}(x,\,k)+\widehat{W}_{S}(x,\,k),\end{split}

(71)

and correspondingly for the Wigner function.

The spin polarization vector of the Dirac field particles with momentum
k
is obtained directly from the particle part of the Wigner function. Choosing a spacelike 3D hypersurface
\Sigma
, the spin polarization of a massive field is obtained from the formula
[
84
,
47
]

S^{\mu}(k)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\;\mathcal{A}_{+}^{\mu}(x,k)}{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\;\mathcal{F}_{+}(x,k)},

(72)

where
\mathcal{F}_{+}
is the scalar particle part of the Wigner function and
\mathcal{A}_{+}^{\mu}
is the axial particle part of the Wigner function. If instead we are considering a massless fermion, its spin polarization vector is given by
[
85
]

S^{\mu}(|\bm{k}|,\bm{k})=\frac{k^{\mu}}{2}\frac{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\;q_{\alpha}\mathcal{A}_{+}^{\alpha}(x,k)}{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\;q_{\alpha}\mathcal{V}_{+}^{\alpha}(x,k)},

(73)

with
\mathcal{V}_{+}^{\mu}
the vector particle part of the Wigner function, and
q
is an arbitrary lightlike vector non orthogonal to
k
, that is
k\cdot q\neq 0
. The result is ultimately independent of the choice of
q

[
85
]
.

It is now straightforward to obtain the first-order gradient expansion of the spin polarization in a fluid described by the statistical operator (
11
): one only needs to apply the gradient expansion developed above for an operator
\widehat{O}
to the Wigner operator. Consider the massive case first. Since the equilibrium operator at point
x
does not have any breaking of parity, the leading order mean value of the axial Wigner operator is simply vanishing:
\langle\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu}(x,\,k)\rangle_{\beta}=0
. The numerator of Eq (
72
) being already of the first order, we do not need to expand the denominator, obtaining:

S^{\mu}(k)\simeq\frac{1}{2}\frac{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\;\Delta\mathcal{A}_{+}^{\mu}(x,k)}{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\;\langle\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{+}(x,k)\rangle_{\beta}}.

(74)

For the massless case we write the more general expansion where we allow the axial part of the Wigner function to have a non-vanishing value at leading order, for instance by including a chiral imbalance in the equilibrium statistical operator as discussed above, and we obtain

\begin{split}S^{\mu}(|\bm{k}|,\,\bm{k})\simeq\frac{k^{\mu}}{2}&\left\{\frac{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\;q_{\alpha}\langle\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\alpha}_{+}(x,k)\rangle_{\beta}}{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\;q_{\alpha}\langle\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\alpha}_{+}(x,k)\rangle_{\beta}}\left[1-\frac{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\;q_{\alpha}\Delta\mathcal{V}_{+}^{\alpha}(x,k)}{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\;q_{\alpha}\langle\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\alpha}_{+}(x,k)\rangle_{\beta}}\right]\right.\\
&\left.+\frac{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\;q_{\alpha}\Delta\mathcal{A}_{+}^{\alpha}(x,k)}{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\;q_{\alpha}\langle\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\alpha}_{+}(x,k)\rangle_{\beta}}\right\}.\end{split}

(75)

In the next sections, all the possible contributions in
\Delta\mathcal{A}_{+}^{\mu}
and
\Delta\mathcal{V}_{+}^{\mu}
induced by first-order hydrodynamic fields will be derived, and the corresponding Kubo formulas will be given. The coefficients will be classified based on the properties of their corresponding correlator under discrete transformations: parity
\mathsf{P}
, time reversal
\mathsf{T}
and charge conjuration
\mathsf{C}
. If the parity of the coefficient under that transformation is even (odd), it also means that the coefficient describes an effect in which a term in our mean value operator (the axial or vector part of the Wigner function) is induced by a hydrodynamic quantity that has the same (opposite) parity under that transformation. As it is expected, all terms that breaks the time reversal symmetry come from the dissipative part of the statistical operator. Coefficients with even (odd) parity under time reversal
\mathsf{T}
are then named non-dissipative (dissipative). Coefficients with even (odd) parity under a parity transformation
\mathsf{P}
are then named non-chiral (chiral), as discussed above. Lastly, coefficients are called charged or not-charged based on charge conjugation
\mathsf{C}
. For charged coefficients, it is important to stress a difference between spin polarization and usual mean values of local operators. The terms in a local operator, such as an electric current
\langle\widehat{j}^{\mu}\rangle
, that are proportional to charged coefficients are usually proportional to the chemical potential
\zeta
. Otherwise, the contribution of the particle part will be canceled by the antiparticle part. However, the spin polarization is related only to the particle part of the Wigner function, and a term proportional to the charged coefficient can be different from zero even at zero chemical potential because there is no antiparticle part to cancel the particle contribution. This is, for instance, the case for the spin Hall effect in spin polarization. An electric field or a gradient of chemical potential is still needed to source the effect, but the coefficient itself, despite being charged, does not vanish at zero chemical potential. The same does not apply to the chiral coefficients, except when the antiparticles have the opposite chirality of the particle.

3
Momentum dependent Kubo formulas

In this section we describe the strategy used to identify and classify all the possible first order contributions to the axial and vector parts of the Wigner function. Consider the linear response of a hydrodynamic field
\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}
of eq. (
26
). One of the advantages of linear response theory is that all thermal expectation values are reduced to thermal averages obtained using the equilibrium statistical operator (
17
). This statistical operator has full
\mathsf{T}^{4}
spacetime translation invariance but not full Lorentz group invariance because the symmetry is broken by the four timelike vector fluid velocity
u(x)
. Nevertheless, the statistical operator has a residual
SO(3)
symmetry, coming from the rotation in the three-dimensional space that leaves invariant the four vector
u
. As a first step,
\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}
is decomposed into its irreducible components of this
SO(3)
group. Each of the hydrodynamic fields
\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}
generally has a scalar
X
, vector
V^{\rho}
, symmetric
\Sigma^{\rho\sigma}
and anti-symmetric
\phi^{\rho\sigma}
rank-2, and rank-3 spin-like
\Phi^{\lambda,\mu\nu}
components. We will also generically denote one of these components with
\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}
.

Consider one such decomposed hydrodynamic field
\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}
. The axial and vector parts of the Wigner function are vectors under Lorentz transformations, see appendix
B
, and depend on the four-vector
k
and on
x
through the hydrodynamic fields. We then write down all possible vectors that we can build with the fluid velocity
u^{\mu}
, the metric tensor
\eta^{\mu\nu}
, the four-momentum
k^{\mu}
, the Levi-Civita symbol
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
and that are linear in
\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}
. These terms correspond to all the possible linear contributions induced by
\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}
. We assign to each of them a coefficient that is also a function of
k
and of
x
, through the equilibrium hydrodynamic fields:
\beta(x)
and
\zeta(x)
. The Kubo formulas for these coefficients are obtained by matching all these terms with the local equilibrium (
42
) and dissipative (
34
) response proportional to
\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}
using the appropriate projector. These coefficients are Lorentz scalars and differ from the usual coefficients related to local operators as they also depend on the momentum
k
. Reminding the general properties under discrete transformations of the main quantities:

\begin{array}[]{|l|cc|cc|}\hline\cr&&&&\\[-10.00002pt]
&\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu}&\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{\mu}&\partial^{\mu}&u^{\mu},\,k^{\mu}\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{P}&(-,+)&(+,-)&(+,-)&(+,-)\\
\mathsf{T}&(+,-)&(+,-)&(-,+)&(+,-)\\
\mathsf{C}&(+,+)&(-,-)&(+,+)&(+,+)\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

(76)

where the first term in the bracket correspond to the component along
u
and the second to the orthogonal part, we can then classify these coefficients based on their properties under these transformations.

Since the identification of all the possible terms contributing to a vector quantity (such as
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\mu}
) and their corresponding Kubo formulas can be obtained independently of the physical meaning of the hydrodynamic field
\mathcal{U}_{(\alpha)}
, the decomposition and the identification of the Kubo formulas for the general case of a scalar
X
, vector
V^{\rho}
, symmetric
\Sigma^{\rho\sigma}
and anti-symmetric
\phi^{\rho\sigma}
rank-2, and rank-3 spin-like
\Phi^{\lambda,\mu\nu}
quantities will be obtained. The results are written for
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
but since the only properties of
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
used is that is a vector, the results are valid for any vector, including
\mathcal{V}^{\mu}
. These results will be then used in section
4
to obtain all the possible terms for the axial and vector parts of the Wigner function resulting from the first order gradient expansion in a general relativistic fluid described with the statistical operator (
11
) and will be classified by their properties. With the exception of including the momentum
k
, the procedure follows the standard method used for gradient expansions in hydrodynamics. Readers familiar with the topic can look through the decompositions (
129
)-(
137
) and proceed to the results in section
5
.

3.1
Scalar

Consider a scalar hydrodynamic field
X(x)
, whose linear response is obtained by the following operators, respectively from the local equilibrium and the dissipative part of the statistical operator:

\widehat{B}_{X}=b_{X}\,X(x)\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{X},\quad\widehat{C}_{X}=c_{X}\,\int_{\Omega}{\rm d}\Omega\,X\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{X},

(77)

where
b_{X}
and
c_{X}
are numbers. From the linear response theory we can factorize
X
and define a function
G^{\mu}_{X}
given by a thermal correlator:

\displaystyle\Delta_{X,\,{\rm LTE}}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle X(x)\,b_{X}\,\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu},\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{X}\right)_{\rm LTE}\equiv X(x)\,G^{\mu}_{X,\,{\rm LTE}},

(78a)

\displaystyle\Delta_{X,\,{\rm D}}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle X(x)\,c_{X}\,\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu},\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{X}\right)_{\rm D}\equiv X(x)\,G^{\mu}_{X,\,{\rm D}}.

(78b)

At first order in
X
, all the possible terms giving a vector are the following:

\begin{split}\Delta_{X}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=&\left[a_{Xu}u^{\mu}+a_{Xk}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]X\equiv G_{X}^{\mu}\,X=\left(G^{\mu}_{X,\,{\rm LTE}}+G^{\mu}_{X,\,{\rm D}}\right)\,X,\end{split}

(79)

and the coefficients are obtained with the following formulas

\displaystyle a_{Xu}=

\displaystyle u_{\mu}G^{\mu}_{X},

\displaystyle a_{Xk}=

\displaystyle\frac{(k\cdot u)k^{\perp}_{\mu}}{k_{\perp}^{2}}G^{\mu}_{X},

(80)

where the definition of a perpendicular vector is given in eq. (
1
).
When
\Delta_{X}\mathcal{A}\cdot k=0
, we must have

a_{Xk}=-a_{Xu}\frac{(k\cdot u)^{2}}{k_{\perp}^{2}}.

(81)

3.2
Vector

The vector component of the irreducible representation under rotation of
u
is a vector hydrodynamic field
V^{\rho}(x)
that is orthogonal to
u
, i.e.,
V\cdot u=0
.
The linear response to
V
is obtained by the following operators, respectively from the local equilibrium and the dissipative part of the density operator:

\widehat{B}_{V}=b_{V}\,V_{\rho}(x)\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{V}^{\rho},\quad\widehat{C}_{V}=c_{V}\,\int_{\Omega}{\rm d}\Omega\,V_{\rho}\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{V}^{\rho}\,.

(82)

The linear response theory gives:

\displaystyle\Delta_{V,\,{\rm LTE}}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle V_{\rho}(x)\,b_{V}\,\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu},\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{V}^{\rho}\right)_{\rm LTE}\equiv V_{\rho}(x)\,G^{\mu\rho}_{V,\,{\rm LTE}},

(83a)

\displaystyle\Delta_{V,\,{\rm D}}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle V_{\rho}(x)\,c_{V}\,\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu},\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{V}^{\rho}\right)_{\rm D}\equiv V_{\rho}(x)\,G^{\mu\rho}_{V,\,{\rm D}}.

(83b)

At first order in
V
, all possible terms resulting in a vector are

\begin{split}\Delta_{V}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=&\left[a_{Vu}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+a_{V\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+a_{Vk}Q^{\mu\rho}+a_{V\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]V_{\rho}\equiv G_{V}^{\mu\rho}\,V_{\rho},\end{split}

(84)

where
\Delta
is defined in eq. (
1
) and
Q
in eq. (
5
).
It is straightforward to show that the coefficients are obtained with the following formulas

\displaystyle a_{Vu}=

\displaystyle\frac{(k\cdot u)}{k_{\perp}^{2}}u_{\mu}k^{\perp}_{\rho}G^{\mu\rho}_{V},

\displaystyle a_{V\Delta}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\Delta_{\mu\rho}G^{\mu\rho}_{V},

(85a)

\displaystyle a_{Vk}=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}Q_{\mu\rho}G^{\mu\rho}_{V},

\displaystyle a_{V\epsilon}=

\displaystyle\frac{(k\cdot u)}{2k_{\perp}^{2}}u^{\lambda}\epsilon_{\lambda\mu\tau\rho}k_{\perp}^{\tau}G^{\mu\rho}_{V}.

(85b)

When
\Delta_{V}\mathcal{A}\cdot k=0
, we must have

a_{Vu}=\frac{2}{3}a_{Vk}-a_{V\Delta}\,.

(86)

3.3
Symmetric tensor

Consider a symmetric tensor hydrodynamic field
\Sigma^{\rho\sigma}(x)
that is orthogonal to
u
and traceless, such as those resulting from the irreducible components decomposition.
Its linear response is obtained by the following operators, respectively from the local equilibrium and the dissipative part of the density operator:

\widehat{B}_{\Sigma}=b_{\Sigma}\,\Sigma_{\rho\sigma}(x)\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\Sigma}^{\rho\sigma},\quad\widehat{C}_{\Sigma}=c_{\Sigma}\,\int_{\Omega}{\rm d}\Omega\,\Sigma_{\rho\sigma}\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\Sigma}^{\rho\sigma}.

(87)

We write the linear response to
\Sigma
to a vector
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
as

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Sigma,\,{\rm LTE}}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\rho\sigma}(x)\,b_{\Sigma}\,\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu},\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\Sigma}^{\rho\sigma}\right)_{\rm LTE}\equiv\Sigma_{\rho\sigma}(x)\,G^{\mu\rho\sigma}_{\Sigma,\,{\rm LTE}},

(88a)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Sigma,\,{\rm D}}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\rho\sigma}(x)\,c_{\Sigma}\,\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu},\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\Sigma}^{\rho\sigma}\right)_{\rm D}\equiv\Sigma_{\rho\sigma}(x)\,G^{\mu\rho\sigma}_{\Sigma,\,{\rm D}},

(88b)

The general form of a vector linear in
\Sigma
is the following:

\begin{split}\Delta_{\Sigma}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=&\left[a_{\Sigma u}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}}{k_{\perp}^{2}}u^{\mu}+a_{\Sigma\Delta}\frac{\Delta^{\mu\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}+a_{\Sigma k}\frac{Q^{\mu\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}+a_{\Sigma\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}\frac{u_{\nu}k^{\perp}_{\alpha}}{(k\cdot u)^{2}}\right]\Sigma_{\rho\sigma}\equiv G_{\Sigma}^{\mu\rho\sigma}\,\Sigma_{\rho\sigma},\end{split}

(89)

and the coefficients are obtained with the following formulas

\displaystyle a_{\Sigma u}=

\displaystyle\frac{u_{\mu}k^{\perp}_{\rho}k^{\perp}_{\sigma}}{k_{\perp}^{2}}G^{\mu\rho\sigma}_{\Sigma},

\displaystyle a_{\Sigma\Delta}=

\displaystyle\frac{(k\cdot u)}{3k_{\perp}^{2}}\Delta_{\mu\rho}k^{\perp}_{\sigma}G^{\mu\rho\sigma}_{\Sigma},

(90a)

\displaystyle a_{\Sigma k}=

\displaystyle\frac{3(k\cdot u)}{2k_{\perp}^{2}}Q_{\mu\rho}k^{\perp}_{\sigma}G^{\mu\rho\sigma}_{\Sigma},

\displaystyle a_{\Sigma\epsilon}=

\displaystyle\frac{(k\cdot u)^{2}}{2(k_{\perp}^{2})^{2}}u^{\lambda}\epsilon_{\lambda\mu\tau\rho}k^{\perp}_{\sigma}k_{\perp}^{\tau}G^{\mu\rho\sigma}_{\Sigma}.

(90b)

When
\Delta_{\Sigma}\mathcal{A}\cdot k=0
, we must have

a_{\Sigma u}=\left(\frac{2}{3}a_{\Sigma k}-a_{\Sigma\Delta}\right)\frac{k^{2}_{\perp}}{(k\cdot u)^{2}}.

(91)

3.4
Antisymmetric tensor

Now, consider an antisymmetric tensor hydrodynamic field
\Gamma^{\rho\sigma}(x)
orthogonal to
u
.
In this case, it is more convenient to decompose it using two vectors orthogonal to
u
:

\Gamma^{\rho\sigma}=\alpha^{\rho}u^{\sigma}-\alpha^{\sigma}u^{\rho}+\phi^{\rho\sigma},

(92)

where

\alpha^{\mu}=u_{\rho}\Gamma^{\mu\rho},\quad\phi^{\rho\sigma}=\Delta^{\rho}_{\,\mu}\Delta^{\sigma}_{\,\nu}\Gamma^{\mu\nu}

(93)

and
\phi
can only be written as

\phi^{\rho\sigma}=\epsilon^{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}\,w_{\mu}\,u_{\nu}.

(94)

Therefore, the linear response of an antisymmetric field can be reduced to the linear response of vectors. Indeed, with the above decomposition, we can rewrite the operators in the statistical operator as

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{\Gamma}=

\displaystyle b_{\Gamma}\,\Gamma_{\rho\sigma}(x)\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma}^{\rho\sigma}=b_{\Gamma}\left(\alpha_{\rho}u_{\sigma}-\alpha_{\sigma}u_{\rho}+\epsilon_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}w^{\mu}u^{\nu}\right)\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma}^{\rho\sigma}=-2b_{\Gamma}\,\alpha_{\rho}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}^{\rho}-2b_{\Gamma}\,w_{\rho}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{w}^{\rho},

(95a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\Gamma}=

\displaystyle c_{\Gamma}\,\int_{\Omega}{\rm d}\Omega\,\Gamma_{\rho\sigma}\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma}^{\rho\sigma}=-2c_{\Gamma}\,\int_{\Omega}{\rm d}\Omega\,\alpha_{\rho}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}^{\rho}-2c_{\Gamma}\,\int_{\Omega}{\rm d}\Omega\,w_{\rho}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{w}^{\rho},

(95b)

where

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}^{\rho}=

\displaystyle u_{\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma}^{\lambda\rho},\quad\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{w}^{\rho}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\rho}u_{\alpha}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma\,\beta\gamma},

(96a)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}^{\rho}=

\displaystyle u_{\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma}^{\lambda\rho},\quad\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{w}^{\rho}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\rho}u_{\alpha}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\Gamma\,\beta\gamma}.

(96b)

Applying the linear response theory to this field, we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Gamma,\,{\rm LTE}}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rho}(x)\,(-2b_{\Gamma})\,\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu},\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}^{\rho}\right)_{\rm LTE}+w_{\rho}(x)\,(-2b_{\Gamma})\,\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu},\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{w}^{\rho}\right)_{\rm LTE}

(97a)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rho}\,G^{\mu\rho}_{\alpha,\,{\rm LTE}}+w_{\rho}\,G^{\mu\rho}_{w,\,{\rm LTE}},

(97b)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Gamma,\,{\rm D}}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rho}(x)\,(-2c_{\Gamma})\,\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu},\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}^{\rho}\right)_{\rm D}+w_{\rho}(x)\,(-2c_{\Gamma})\,\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mu},\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{w}^{\rho}\right)_{\rm D}

(97c)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rho}\,G^{\mu\rho}_{\alpha,\,{\rm D}}+w_{\rho}\,G^{\mu\rho}_{w,\,{\rm D}}.

(97d)

Using the results obtained above for the vector part, we obtain all the possible terms linear to
\Gamma
:

\Delta_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=\Delta_{\alpha}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}+\Delta_{w}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=G^{\mu\rho}_{\alpha}\alpha_{\rho}+G^{\mu\rho}_{w}w_{\rho},

(98)

and

\displaystyle\Delta_{\alpha}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle\left[a_{\alpha\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}-a_{\alpha u}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+a_{\alpha k}Q^{\mu\rho}+a_{\alpha\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]\alpha_{\rho}\equiv G_{\alpha}^{\mu\rho}\,\alpha_{\rho},

(99a)

\displaystyle\Delta_{w}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle\left[a_{w\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}-a_{wu}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+a_{wk}Q^{\mu\rho}+a_{w\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]w_{\rho}\equiv G_{w}^{\mu\rho}\,w_{\rho},

(99b)

with the Kubo formulas

\displaystyle a_{\alpha u}=

\displaystyle-\frac{(k\cdot u)}{k_{\perp}^{2}}u_{\mu}k^{\perp}_{\rho}G^{\mu\rho}_{\alpha},

\displaystyle a_{\alpha\Delta}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\Delta_{\mu\rho}G^{\mu\rho}_{\alpha},

(100a)

\displaystyle a_{\alpha k}=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}Q_{\mu\rho}G^{\mu\rho}_{\alpha},

\displaystyle a_{\alpha\epsilon}=

\displaystyle\frac{(k\cdot u)}{2k_{\perp}^{2}}u^{\lambda}\epsilon_{\lambda\mu\tau\rho}k_{\perp}^{\tau}G^{\mu\rho}_{\alpha},

(100b)

and

\displaystyle a_{wu}=

\displaystyle-\frac{(k\cdot u)}{k_{\perp}^{2}}u_{\mu}k^{\perp}_{\rho}G^{\mu\rho}_{w},

\displaystyle a_{w\Delta}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\Delta_{\mu\rho}G^{\mu\rho}_{w},

(101a)

\displaystyle a_{wk}=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}Q_{\mu\rho}G^{\mu\rho}_{w},

\displaystyle a_{w\epsilon}=

\displaystyle\frac{(k\cdot u)}{2k_{\perp}^{2}}u^{\lambda}\epsilon_{\lambda\mu\tau\rho}k_{\perp}^{\tau}G^{\mu\rho}_{w}.

(101b)

When
\Delta_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A}\cdot k=0
, we must have

a_{\alpha k}=\frac{3}{2}\left(a_{\alpha u}+a_{\alpha\Delta}\right),\quad a_{wk}=\frac{3}{2}\left(a_{wu}+a_{w\Delta}\right).

(102)

3.5
Rank-3 spin-like tensor

Lastly, we consider a hydrodynamic field
\Phi^{\lambda,\mu\nu}
with
\Phi^{\lambda,\mu\nu}=-\Phi^{\lambda,\nu\mu}
, which has 24 degrees of freedom. We first need to decompose it in the irreducible representation under the
SO(3)
group that leaves
u
invariant.
To this scope, we project the tensor onto the vector
u
and, taking the antisymmetry of
\mu\nu
indices into account, we obtain

\Phi^{\lambda,\mu\nu}=u^{\lambda}\Gamma^{\mu\nu}+I^{\lambda\mu}u^{\nu}-I^{\lambda\nu}u^{\mu}+\Phi^{\langle\lambda\rangle,\langle\mu\rangle\langle\nu\rangle},

(103)

where

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle u_{\lambda}\Phi^{\lambda\mu\nu},

(104a)

\displaystyle I^{\lambda\mu}=

\displaystyle\Delta^{\lambda}_{\,\tau}\Delta^{\mu}_{\,\rho}u_{\sigma}\Phi^{\tau\rho\sigma},

(104b)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\langle\lambda\rangle,\langle\mu\rangle\langle\nu\rangle}=

\displaystyle\Delta^{\lambda}_{\,\tau}\Delta^{\mu}_{\,\rho}\Delta^{\nu}_{\,\sigma}\Phi^{\tau\rho\sigma}.

(104c)

The antisymmetric tensor
\Gamma^{\mu\nu}
can be decomposed into:

\Gamma^{\mu\nu}=f^{\mu}u^{\nu}-f^{\nu}u^{\mu}+\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}u_{\alpha}\Upsilon_{\beta},

(105)

where

f^{\mu}=u_{\lambda}u_{\nu}\Phi^{\lambda\mu\nu},\quad\Upsilon^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}u^{\tau}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}u_{\nu}\Phi_{\tau,\rho\sigma}.

(106)

The rank-2 tensor
I^{\lambda\mu}
orthogonal to
u
, can be decomposed in a scalar part (the trace), an antisymmetric tensor and a symmetric traceless tensor:

I^{\lambda\mu}=I\Delta^{\lambda\mu}+I_{A}^{\lambda\mu}+I_{S}^{\lambda\mu}=I\Delta^{\lambda\mu}+\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\alpha\beta}u_{\alpha}I_{\beta}+I_{S}^{\lambda\mu},

(107)

where

\displaystyle I=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\Delta_{\lambda\mu}\Delta^{\lambda}_{\,\tau}\Delta^{\mu}_{\,\rho}u_{\sigma}\Phi^{\tau\rho\sigma}=\frac{1}{3}\Delta_{\tau\rho}u_{\sigma}\Phi^{\tau\rho\sigma},

(108a)

\displaystyle I_{A}^{\lambda\mu}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\Delta^{\lambda}_{\,\tau}\Delta^{\mu}_{\,\rho}-\Delta^{\mu}_{\,\tau}\Delta^{\lambda}_{\,\rho}\right)u_{\sigma}\Phi^{\tau\rho\sigma},\quad I^{\mu}=-\frac{1}{2}u^{\rho}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\tau\sigma}u_{\nu}\Phi_{\tau,\rho\sigma},

(108b)

\displaystyle I_{S}^{\lambda\mu}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\Delta^{\lambda}_{\,\tau}\Delta^{\mu}_{\,\rho}+\Delta^{\mu}_{\,\tau}\Delta^{\lambda}_{\,\rho}-\frac{2}{3}\Delta_{\tau\rho}\Delta^{\lambda\mu}\right)u_{\sigma}\Phi^{\tau\rho\sigma}.

(108c)

Furthermore,
\Phi^{\langle\lambda\rangle,\langle\mu\rangle\langle\nu\rangle}
can be decomposed in a completely anti-symmetric part, a part symmetric in the exchange of first and second indices and a part symmetric in the exchange of first and third indices:

\Phi^{\langle\lambda\rangle,\langle\mu\rangle\langle\nu\rangle}=\phi\,\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\nu\rho}u_{\rho}+\Phi_{S12}^{\lambda,\mu\nu}+\Phi_{S13}^{\lambda,\mu\nu}

(109)

obtained with

\displaystyle\phi=

\displaystyle-\frac{u^{\rho}}{6}\epsilon_{\lambda\mu\nu\rho}\Phi^{\lambda,\mu\nu},

(110a)

\displaystyle\Phi_{S12}^{\lambda,\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\left[\Delta^{\lambda}_{\rho}\Delta^{\mu}_{\sigma}\Delta^{\nu}_{\alpha}+\Delta^{\mu}_{\rho}\Delta^{\lambda}_{\sigma}\Delta^{\nu}_{\alpha}-\Delta^{\nu}_{\rho}\Delta^{\mu}_{\sigma}\Delta^{\lambda}_{\alpha}-\Delta^{\mu}_{\rho}\Delta^{\nu}_{\sigma}\Delta^{\lambda}_{\alpha}\right]\Phi^{\rho,\sigma\alpha},

(110b)

\displaystyle\Phi_{S13}^{\lambda,\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\left[\Delta^{\lambda}_{\rho}\Delta^{\mu}_{\sigma}\Delta^{\nu}_{\alpha}+\Delta^{\nu}_{\rho}\Delta^{\mu}_{\sigma}\Delta^{\lambda}_{\alpha}-\Delta^{\mu}_{\rho}\Delta^{\lambda}_{\sigma}\Delta^{\nu}_{\alpha}-\Delta^{\mu}_{\rho}\Delta^{\nu}_{\sigma}\Delta^{\lambda}_{\alpha}\right]\Phi^{\rho,\sigma\alpha}.

(110c)

All together, we have

\begin{split}\Phi^{\lambda,\mu\nu}=&u^{\lambda}\left(f^{\mu}u^{\nu}-f^{\nu}u^{\mu}\right)+u^{\lambda}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}u_{\alpha}\Upsilon_{\beta}+\left(\Delta^{\lambda\mu}u^{\nu}-\Delta^{\lambda\nu}u^{\mu}\right)I\\
&+\left(\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\alpha\beta}u^{\nu}-\epsilon^{\lambda\nu\alpha\beta}u^{\mu}\right)u_{\alpha}I_{\beta}+\left(I_{S}^{\lambda\mu}u^{\nu}-I_{S}^{\lambda\nu}u^{\mu}\right)+\phi\,\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\nu\rho}u_{\rho}+\Phi_{S12}^{\lambda,\mu\nu}+\Phi_{S13}^{\lambda,\mu\nu}.\end{split}

(111)

One can check that the count of the degrees of freedom is, from left to right,
24=3+3+1+3+5+1+4+4
. The Schouten identity allow us to write the factor in front of
\Upsilon_{\beta}
as

u^{\lambda}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}=u^{\mu}\epsilon^{\lambda\alpha\beta\nu}+u^{\nu}\epsilon^{\mu\alpha\beta\lambda}-u^{\alpha}\epsilon^{\beta\lambda\mu\nu}+u^{\beta}\epsilon^{\mu\lambda\nu\alpha},

(112)

then, using
\Upsilon\cdot u=0
, we have

u^{\lambda}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}u_{\alpha}\Upsilon_{\beta}=\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\nu\rho}\Upsilon_{\rho}-\left(u^{\nu}\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\alpha\beta}-u^{\mu}\epsilon^{\lambda\nu\alpha\beta}\right)u_{\alpha}\Upsilon_{\beta},

(113)

and we can write

\begin{split}\Phi^{\lambda,\mu\nu}=&u^{\lambda}\left(f^{\mu}u^{\nu}-f^{\nu}u^{\mu}\right)+\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\nu\rho}\Upsilon_{\rho}+\left(\Delta^{\lambda\mu}u^{\nu}-\Delta^{\lambda\nu}u^{\mu}\right)I\\
&+\left(\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\alpha\beta}u^{\nu}-\epsilon^{\lambda\nu\alpha\beta}u^{\mu}\right)u_{\alpha}(I_{\beta}-\Upsilon_{\beta})+\left(I_{S}^{\lambda\mu}u^{\nu}-I_{S}^{\lambda\nu}u^{\mu}\right)\\
&+\phi\,\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\nu\rho}u_{\rho}+\Phi_{S12}^{\lambda,\mu\nu}+\Phi_{S13}^{\lambda,\mu\nu}.\end{split}

(114)

This form is more convenient when
\Phi^{\lambda,\mu\nu}
is completely antisymmetric, e.g. the canonical spin tensor. In this case
\Phi^{\lambda,\mu\nu}
has only 4 degree of freedom and has
I^{\mu}=\Upsilon^{\mu}
, such that the decomposition simplify in

\Phi^{\{\lambda,\mu\nu\}}=\phi\,\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\nu\rho}u_{\rho}+\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\nu\rho}\Upsilon_{\rho}.

(115)

Using the decomposition (
114
), the local thermal equilibrium and dissipative operators in the statistical operator proportional to a hydrodynamic field
\Phi^{\lambda,\mu\nu}
are also decomposed as

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{\Phi}=

\displaystyle b_{\Phi}\,\Phi_{\tau,\rho\sigma}(x)\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\Phi}^{\tau,\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2b_{\Phi}f_{\rho}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{f}^{\rho}-2b_{\Phi}\Upsilon_{\rho}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\Upsilon}^{\rho}+2b_{\Phi}\,I\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{I}-2b_{\Phi}(I_{\rho}-\Upsilon_{\rho})\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{I-\Upsilon}^{\rho}+2b_{\Phi}I_{S\,\rho\sigma}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{I_{S}}^{\rho\sigma}

(116a)

\displaystyle+b_{\Phi}\,\phi\,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\phi}+3b_{\Phi}\Phi^{\rm S12}_{\tau,\rho\sigma}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\rm S12}^{\tau,\rho\sigma}+3b_{\Phi}\Phi^{\rm S13}_{\tau,\rho\sigma}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\rm S13}^{\tau,\rho\sigma},

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\Phi}=

\displaystyle c_{\Phi}\,\int_{\Omega}{\rm d}\Omega\,\Phi_{\tau,\rho\sigma}\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\Phi}^{\tau,\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}{\rm d}\Omega\left[2c_{\Phi}f_{\rho}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{f}^{\rho}-2c_{\phi}\Upsilon_{\rho}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\Upsilon}^{\rho}+2c_{\Phi}\,I\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{I}-2c_{\Phi}(I_{\rho}-\Upsilon_{\rho})\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{I-\Upsilon}^{\rho}+2c_{\Phi}I_{S\,\rho\sigma}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{I_{S}}^{\rho\sigma}\right.

(116b)

\displaystyle\left.+c_{\Phi}\,\phi\,\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\phi}+3c_{\Phi}\Phi^{\rm S12}_{\tau,\rho\sigma}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm S12}^{\tau,\rho\sigma}+3c_{\Phi}\Phi^{\rm S13}_{\tau,\rho\sigma}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\rm S13}^{\tau,\rho\sigma}\right],

where

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{f}^{\rho}=

\displaystyle u^{\lambda}\Delta^{\mu\rho}u^{\nu}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\Phi}_{\lambda,\mu\nu},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\Upsilon}^{\rho}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\rho\lambda\mu\nu}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\Phi}_{\lambda,\mu\nu},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{I}=

\displaystyle\Delta^{\lambda\mu}u^{\nu}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\Phi}_{\lambda,\mu\nu},

(117a)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{f}^{\rho}=

\displaystyle u^{\lambda}\Delta^{\mu\rho}u^{\nu}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\Phi}_{\lambda,\mu\nu},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\Upsilon}^{\rho}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\rho\lambda\mu\nu}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\Phi}_{\lambda,\mu\nu},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{I}=

\displaystyle\Delta^{\lambda\mu}u^{\nu}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\Phi}_{\lambda,\mu\nu},

(117b)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{I-\Upsilon}^{\rho}=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\rho\lambda\mu\alpha}u_{\alpha}u^{\nu}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\Phi}_{\lambda,\mu\nu},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{I_{S}}^{\rho\sigma}=

\displaystyle\Delta^{\lambda\rho}\Delta^{\sigma\mu}u^{\nu}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\Phi}_{\lambda,\mu\nu},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\phi}=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\nu\rho}u_{\rho}\widehat{\mathcal{B}}^{\Phi}_{\lambda,\mu\nu},

(117c)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{I-\Upsilon}^{\rho}=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\rho\lambda\mu\alpha}u_{\alpha}u^{\nu}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\Phi}_{\lambda,\mu\nu},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{I_{S}}^{\rho\sigma}=

\displaystyle\Delta^{\lambda\rho}\Delta^{\sigma\mu}u^{\nu}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{\Phi}_{\lambda,\mu\nu},
