As we might have expected, the gradients of spin potential are the only hydrodynamic fields capable of generating dissipative effects on the axial part of the Wigner function that do not require a breaking of parity.

5
Results and discussion

This section presents the results and discusses them.

5.1
The axial part of Wigner function up to first order in hydrodynamic gradients

The previous sections obtained all the linear contributions of the particle axial part of the Wigner function, which is connected to the spin polarization through eq. (
72
). The decomposition and the definitions of the hydrodynamic fields that can induce polarization are given in eqs. (
129
)-(
137
) and the associated Kubo formula are explicitly written through section
4
. To present the results, all possible contributions are divided into four categories based on two properties: whether they are dissipative contributions and whether they require a breaking of the parity symmetry. In general, there are 13 non-dissipative and non-chiral possible contributions to the axial Wigner function:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm LTE}\mathcal{A}_{+}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle\left[a_{\zeta_{A}u}u^{\mu}+a_{\zeta_{A}k}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]\zeta_{A}+a^{c}_{r\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\partial_{\langle\rho\rangle}\zeta

(207a)

\displaystyle-a_{\varpi}\frac{2{\widetilde{\varpi}}^{\mu\nu}k_{\nu}}{(2\pi)^{3}}-(a_{wu}-a_{w\Delta})\frac{(k\cdot w)}{(k\cdot u)}u^{\mu}

(207b)

\displaystyle+(a_{\alpha\epsilon}-a_{w\Delta})\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\alpha_{\rho}+a_{wk}Q^{\mu\rho}w_{\rho}

(207c)

\displaystyle-a_{\mathfrak{S}-\varpi}\frac{2({\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\mu\nu}-{\widetilde{\varpi}}^{\mu\nu})k_{\nu}}{(2\pi)^{3}}-(a_{\mathfrak{w}-wu}-a_{\mathfrak{w}-w\Delta})\frac{k^{\rho}_{\perp}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}(\mathfrak{w}-w)_{\rho}

(207d)

\displaystyle+(a_{\mathfrak{a}-\alpha\epsilon}-a_{\mathfrak{w}-w\Delta})\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}(\mathfrak{a}-\alpha)_{\rho}+a_{\mathfrak{w}-wk}Q^{\mu\rho}(\mathfrak{w}-w)_{\rho}

(207e)

\displaystyle+a_{q\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}(\beta Du_{\rho}+\partial_{\rho}\beta)+a_{\sigma\epsilon}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}\frac{u_{\nu}k_{\alpha}}{(k\cdot u)}\beta\sigma_{\rho\sigma},

(207f)

and 15 non-dissipative and chiral coefficients

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\rm LTE},\chi}\mathcal{A}_{+}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{r_{A}\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\partial_{\langle\rho\rangle}\zeta_{A}

(208a)

\displaystyle+\left[\mathfrak{a}_{fu}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\mathfrak{a}_{f\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+\mathfrak{a}_{fk}Q^{\mu\rho}\right]f_{\rho}+\mathfrak{a}_{\Upsilon\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\Upsilon_{\rho}

(208b)

\displaystyle+\left[\mathfrak{a}_{Iu}u^{\mu}+\mathfrak{a}_{Ik}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]I+\mathfrak{a}_{I-\Upsilon\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}(I_{\rho}-\Upsilon_{\rho})

(208c)

\displaystyle+\left[\mathfrak{a}_{I_{S}u}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}}{k_{\perp}^{2}}u^{\mu}+\mathfrak{a}_{I_{S}\Delta}\frac{\Delta^{\mu\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}+\mathfrak{a}_{I_{S}k}\frac{Q^{\mu\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]I_{S\,\rho\sigma}

(208d)

\displaystyle+\left[\mathfrak{a}_{\rm{S12}\Delta}\Delta^{\tau\rho}\Delta^{\mu\sigma}+\mathfrak{a}_{\rm{S12}k}Q^{\tau\rho}\Delta^{\mu\sigma}\right]\Phi^{S12}_{\tau,\rho\sigma}

(208e)

\displaystyle+\left[\mathfrak{a}_{\rm{S13}\Delta}\Delta^{\tau\sigma}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+\mathfrak{a}_{\rm{S13}k}Q^{\tau\sigma}\Delta^{\mu\rho}\right]\Phi^{S13}_{\tau,\rho\sigma}.

(208f)

Regarding dissipative contributions, 19 non-chiral possible contributions were found

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm D}\mathcal{A}_{+}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle\left[\bar{a}_{D\zeta_{A}u}u^{\mu}+\bar{a}_{D\zeta_{A}k}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]D\zeta_{A}+\left[\bar{a}_{r_{A}u}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\bar{a}_{r_{A}\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+\bar{a}_{r_{A}k}Q^{\mu\rho}\right]\partial_{\langle\rho\rangle}\zeta_{A}

(209a)

\displaystyle+\bar{a}_{f\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}f_{\rho}+\left[\bar{a}_{\Upsilon u}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\bar{a}_{\Upsilon\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+\bar{a}_{\Upsilon k}Q^{\mu\rho}\right]\Upsilon_{\rho}

(209b)

\displaystyle+\left[\bar{a}_{I-\Upsilon u}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\bar{a}_{I-\Upsilon\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+\bar{a}_{I-\Upsilon k}Q^{\mu\rho}\right](I_{\rho}-\Upsilon_{\rho})

(209c)

\displaystyle+\left[\bar{a}_{\phi u}u^{\mu}+\bar{a}_{\phi k}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]\phi+\bar{a}_{I_{S}\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}\frac{u_{\nu}k^{\perp}_{\alpha}}{(k\cdot u)^{2}}I_{S\,\rho\sigma}

(209d)

\displaystyle+\left[\bar{a}_{\rm{S12}\Delta\epsilon}\Delta^{\mu\tau}\epsilon^{\lambda\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{u_{\lambda}k^{\perp}_{\nu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\bar{a}_{\rm{S12}\epsilon}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\tau}u_{\nu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]\Phi^{S12}_{\tau,\rho\sigma}

(209e)

\displaystyle+\left[\bar{a}_{\rm{S13}\Delta\epsilon}\Delta^{\mu\tau}\epsilon^{\lambda\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{u_{\lambda}k^{\perp}_{\nu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\bar{a}_{\rm{S13}\epsilon}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\tau}u_{\nu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]\Phi^{S13}_{\tau,\rho\sigma},

(209f)

and 19 possible chiral contributions

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\rm D},\chi}\mathcal{A}_{+}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle\left[\bar{\mathfrak{a}}^{c}_{D\zeta u}u^{\mu}+\bar{\mathfrak{a}}^{c}_{D\zeta k}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]D\zeta+\left[\bar{\mathfrak{a}}^{c}_{ru}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\bar{\mathfrak{a}}^{c}_{r\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+\bar{\mathfrak{a}}^{c}_{rk}Q^{\mu\rho}\right]\partial_{\langle\rho\rangle}\zeta

(210a)

\displaystyle+\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{a}-\alpha\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}(\mathfrak{a}-\alpha)_{\rho}-\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{a}-\alpha u}\frac{k^{\rho}_{\perp}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}(\mathfrak{a}-\alpha)_{\rho}+\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{w}-w\epsilon}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}(\mathfrak{w}-w)_{\rho}

(210b)

\displaystyle+\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{\mathfrak{a}-\alpha k}Q^{\mu\rho}(\mathfrak{a}-\alpha)_{\rho}+\left[\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{D\beta u}u^{\mu}+\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{D\beta k}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]D\beta+\left[\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{\theta u}u^{\mu}+\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{\theta k}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]\beta\theta

(210c)

\displaystyle+\left[\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{qu}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{q\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{qk}Q^{\mu\rho}\right](\beta Du_{\rho}+\partial_{\rho}\beta)+\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{\sigma u}(k\cdot u)\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}}{k_{\perp}^{2}}u^{\mu}\,\beta\sigma_{\rho\sigma}

(210d)

\displaystyle+\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{\sigma\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}\beta\sigma_{\rho\sigma}+\bar{\mathfrak{a}}_{\sigma k}Q^{\mu\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}\,\beta\sigma_{\rho\sigma}.

(210e)

The non-dissipative, non-chiral contributions given in eq. (
207
) have already been obtained in previous works. The first terms in eq. (
207a
) describe a spin polarization induced by a chiral imbalance and, for a free field, is given by
[
89
]

a_{\zeta_{A}k}^{\rm Free}=\frac{g_{A}}{2}\frac{2(k\cdot u)}{(2\pi)^{3}}\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)n_{F}(\beta\cdot k)\left(1-n_{F}(\beta\cdot k)\right)=-\frac{(k\cdot u)^{2}}{k_{\perp}^{2}}a_{\zeta_{A}u}^{\rm Free},

(211)

where
g_{A}
is the axial charge of the baryon species. Taking advantage of this effect, it is possible to probe the presence of a chiral imbalance in the QGP by measuring the correlation between helicities of particles emitted in the same event
[
89
,
90
]
. The last terms in eq. (
207a
) is the spin Hall effect
[
23
,
79
]
and for a non-interacting Dirac field is given by:

a_{r\epsilon}^{c{\rm Free}}=-\frac{2\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)}{(2\pi)^{3}}n_{F}(\beta\cdot k)\left(1-n_{F}(\beta\cdot k)\right),

(212)

and we will discuss this effect further in the next section. The contributions induced by thermal vorticity are given in the lines (
207b
) and (
207c
) and are the only one surviving at global equilibrium. For a free field they were first obtained in
[
5
]
, and they result in

\displaystyle a^{\rm Free}_{\varpi}=

\displaystyle\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)n_{F}(\beta\cdot k)\left(1-n_{F}(\beta\cdot k)\right),

(213a)

\displaystyle a^{\rm Free}_{wu}=

\displaystyle a^{\rm Free}_{w\Delta},\quad a^{\rm Free}_{\alpha\epsilon}=a^{\rm Free}_{w\Delta},\quad a^{\rm Free}_{wk}=0.

(213b)

It is interesting to note that among all the four possible terms in eqs. (
207b
) and (
207c
), only the first one is non-vanishing for non-interacting particles. Interactions can break the degeneracy of the coefficients and give rise to the additional contributions. These additional effects and their Kubo formulas were first obtained in
[
21
]
.
The contributions coming from the spin potential are given in eqs. (
207d
) and (
207e
). In contrast to the previous coefficients, those related to the spin potential depend on the pseudo-gauge chosen to express the energy-momentum tensor and the spin tensor in the statistical operator. For a non-interacting Dirac field, choosing the Canonical spin tensor, one obtains
[
56
,
78
]

\begin{split}\Delta_{\mathfrak{S}-\varpi,{\rm LTE}}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}_{+}=&\epsilon^{\lambda\rho\sigma\tau}u_{\tau}\frac{\Delta^{\mu}_{\tau}k^{2}-k^{\mu}k_{\tau}}{(k\cdot u)}(\mathfrak{S}_{\rho\sigma}-\varpi_{\rho\sigma})\\
&\times\frac{\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)}{(2\pi)^{3}}n_{F}(\beta\cdot k)\left(1-n_{F}(\beta\cdot k)\right),\end{split}

(214)

from which one can obtain the different coefficients. Lastly, the thermal shear induced polarization is given in eq. (
207f
). These coefficients are also pseudo-gauge dependent
[
56
]
, and they were first obtained in
[
21
,
22
]
. For a free Dirac field the result is the same in the Canonical and the Belinfante pseudo-gauge:

a_{\sigma\epsilon}^{\rm Free}=\frac{2\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)}{(2\pi)^{3}}n_{F}(\beta\cdot k)\left(1-n_{F}(\beta\cdot k)\right)=\frac{2a_{q\epsilon}^{\rm Free}}{(k\cdot u)},

(215)

instead in the de Groot–van Leeuwen–van Weert or the Hilgevoord-Wouthuysen pseudo-gauge the coefficients are vanishing
[
56
]
. We stress again that despite these results were already known, we showed that at first order in gradients these are all the possible contributions and the coefficients are given by Kubo formulas valid for any quantum field theory. We leave for future studies the possible phenomenological consequences of considering the spin polarization in an interacting fluid.

To the best of our knowledge, the non-dissipative chiral contributions to spin polarization are given in eq. (
208
) for the first time. The first term is the chiral analogue of the spin Hall effect and the other are induced by the gradient of the spin potential. Moreover, this is also the first time that the dissipative contributions of spin polarization, (
209
) and (
210
), have been identified. It is important to stress that dissipative effects induced by thermal shear, which include the expansion rate, the shear tensor, gradients of temperature, and of fluid velocity, are only possible in the presence of parity symmetry breaking, either through interactions or by the presence of chiral imbalance. For this reason one expect these contributions to be small and therefore not of phenomenological relevance. The only effects possible without parity breaking or a gradient of an axial chemical potential are those induced by the gradient of spin potential in eq. (
209
). Among the non-dissipative coefficients, only the one related to the gradients of the vector and axial chemical potential are independent of the pseudo-gauge chosen.

5.2
Chiral spin Hall effect

Figure 2:
The figure illustrates the effects similar to the spin Hall effect (SHE) for the spin polarization of a particle with momentum
k
in the
z
direction. In purple along the
y
direction, the hydrodynamic fields inducing the effects. The effects on the top part induce a contribution to the axial part of the Wigner function, which is related to spin polarization
S
. The effects on the bottom generate terms in the vector part of the Wigner function
\mathcal{J}
, which is related to spin polarization of massless fields. The symbol
\chi
in the material indicates that the coefficient related to the effect require a breaking of parity. Replacing
k
with a magnetic field
B
, the hydrodynamic fields with an electric field
E
, and
S
and
\mathcal{J}
with an electric potential, we obtain the classical Hall effect.

Within the non-dissipative effects we discuss in more depth the analogues of the spin Hall effect (SHE). The SHE describe a spin polarization induced by the gradient of
\zeta
. Since in a system embedded in an external electromagnetic field the thermal global equilibrium is reached if the comoving electric field equals the gradient of
\zeta
, that is
\partial^{\mu}\zeta(x)=F^{\nu\mu}\beta_{\nu}
, a spin polarization is induced in the same way by an electric field. In heavy-ion collisions the presence of the SHE can be detected by looking at the azimuthal angle behavior of the difference between the local spin polarization of
\Lambda
s and
\bar{\Lambda}
s
[
23
]
. Indeed, the SHE conductivity
a^{c}_{r\epsilon}
is a charged coefficients and give opposite effect for the
\Lambda
and the
\bar{\Lambda}
. The analysis carried out in this work revealed that the gradients of
\zeta
and
\zeta_{A}
can induce similar effects in both the axial and vector part of the Wigner function. For the axial part of Wigner function we found that they induce the following terms

\Delta_{\rm SHE}\mathcal{A}_{+}^{\mu}(x,k)=\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\left[a^{c}_{r\epsilon}(k)\partial_{\rho}\zeta+\mathfrak{a}_{r_{A}\epsilon}(k)\partial_{\rho}\zeta_{A}\right].

These are relevant for the spin polarization of a massive field, see eq. (
72
). We call the axial Wigner function induced by
\partial_{\rho}\zeta_{A}
the chiral spin Hall effect (CSHE), its corresponding coefficients is chiral and is sourced by gradient of the axial chemical potential. Similarly to the spin polarization induced by the axial chemical potential in eq. (
207a
), these effect could be used to detect anisotropies of the axial chemical potential, deriving from the topological states of the QCD vacuum, by looking at the local spin polarization or helicity. For a massless field, also the vector part of the Wigner function play a role in spin polarization, see eq. (
73
). The gradients of the chemical potentials induce the following terms:

\Delta_{\rm SHE}\mathcal{V}_{+}^{\mu}(x,k)=\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\left[\mathfrak{v}_{r\epsilon}(k)\partial_{\rho}\zeta+v^{c}_{r_{A}\epsilon}(k)\partial_{\rho}\zeta_{A}\right].

Similarly to the chiral magnetic effect, we call the vector part of the Wigner function induced by the gradient of
\zeta
, or by an electric field, the chiral electrical effect (CEE). The corresponding coefficient
\mathfrak{v}_{r\epsilon}
is chiral. We call the term induced by
\partial_{\rho}\zeta_{A}
the axial Hall effect (AHE), whose coefficient
v^{c}_{r_{A}\epsilon}
is not chiral, but it requires a non-homogeneous chiral imbalance to generate the effect. The SHE is the only effect that does not require a breaking of parity. Instead, all of them are non-dissipative and require the fluid to be out-of-equilibrium. A summary of the effects is given in figure
2
.
To obtain a spin current the corrections to the axial Wigner function must be plugged in the expression for the spin vector (
72
) obtaining in the rest frame of the system:

\vec{S}(k)=\vec{k}\times\left\langle\left\langle a_{r\epsilon}^{c}\left(\frac{\vec{\nabla}\mu}{T}+\mu\vec{\nabla}\frac{1}{T}\right)\right\rangle\right\rangle+\vec{k}\times\left\langle\left\langle\mathfrak{a}_{r_{A}\epsilon}\left(\frac{\vec{\nabla}\mu_{A}}{T}+\mu_{A}\vec{\nabla}\frac{1}{T}\right)\right\rangle\right\rangle.

(216)

In the context of spintronics
[
91
]
, the CSHE refer to the generation of a spin current induced by the inherent chirality of the material—stemming from structural properties
[
92
]
(like the chiral induced Spin Selectivity Effect (CISS)
[
93
]
and the Quantum Spin Hall Effect (QSHE)
[
94
]
), magnetic configurations
[
95
]
(like the Topological Spin Hall Effect (TSHE)
[
96
]
, or atomic vibrations (chiral phonons)
[
97
]
. The CSHE has been observed in diverse materials including HgTe quantum wells
[
98
]
, van der Waals materials like WTe2
[
99
]
, chiral organic molecules such as DNA
[
93
]
, ferromagnetic metals with static skyrmion lattices
[
100
]
, and 2D hybrid organic-inorganic perovskites
[
97
]
.

The values of these coefficients can be calculated using the Kubo formulas in eqs. (
149d
) and (
149e
). In a fluid with a chiral imbalance
\zeta_{A}(x)
and a chemical potential
\zeta(x)
, these coefficients have been computed in appendix
C
for a massless non-interacting Dirac field or equivalently for the high temperature limit
T\gg m
. The results are, for the SHE and the CSHE:

\displaystyle a^{c}_{r\epsilon}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta(k^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[n^{R}_{F}(x,k)(1-n^{R}_{F}(x,k))+n^{L}_{F}(x,k)(1-n^{L}_{F}(x,k))\right],

(217)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{r_{A}\epsilon}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta(k^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[n^{R}_{F}(x,k)(1-n^{R}_{F}(x,k))-n^{L}_{F}(x,k)(1-n^{L}_{F}(x,k))\right],

(218)

and for the CEE and the AHE:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}_{r\epsilon}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta(k^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[n^{R}_{F}(x,k)(1-n^{R}_{F}(x,k))-n^{L}_{F}(x,k)(1-n^{L}_{F}(x,k))\right],

(219)

\displaystyle v^{c}_{r_{A}\epsilon}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta(k^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[n^{R}_{F}(x,k)(1-n^{R}_{F}(x,k))+n^{L}_{F}(x,k)(1-n^{L}_{F}(x,k))\right],

(220)

where

n^{\chi}_{F}(x,k)=\frac{1}{{\rm e}^{\beta(x)\cdot k-\zeta(x)-\chi\zeta_{A}(x)}+1},\quad\chi=\begin{cases}+1&R\\
-1&L\\
\end{cases}\,.

(221)

We note that in the absence of chiral imbalance the SHE conductivity reduces to the know value in eq. (
212
), and that the chiral coefficients
\mathfrak{a}_{r_{A}\epsilon}
and
\mathfrak{v}_{r\epsilon}
and the non-chiral coefficients
a^{c}_{r\epsilon}
and
v^{c}_{r_{A}\epsilon}
have the same values. For the chiral coefficients we can expect that their values are connected to the chiral anomaly. An other question is whether these effects survives in the axial and vector currents obtained by integration the Wigner function over the momentum
k
. In the local rest frame, by noticing that the coefficients depend only on the modulus
|\bm{k}|
and that the effects are odd in
k_{\perp}
, we conclude that these effect do not generate a net axial and vector current, that is

\Delta_{\rm SHE}\widehat{j}^{\mu}_{A}(x)=\int{\rm d}^{4}k\,\Delta_{\rm SHE}\mathcal{A}^{\mu}(x,k)=0,\quad\Delta_{\rm SHE}\widehat{j}^{\mu}(x)=\int{\rm d}^{4}k\,\Delta_{\rm SHE}\mathcal{V}^{\mu}(x,k)=0\,.

(222)

Similarly, these effects can only be detected in the local spin polarization, that is as a function of momentum, and do not appear in the global spin polarization, the one averaged over the momenta of all particles.

5.3
Spin polarization of a free massless fermion

Now, consider the spin polarization of a massless fermion given by eq. (
73
).
At first order in gradient expansion the spin polarization becomes eq. (
75
) or in compact notation

S^{\mu}(|\bm{k}|,\bm{k})=\frac{k^{\mu}}{2}\left[\frac{A_{0}}{V_{0}}\left(1-\frac{\Delta V}{V_{0}}\right)+\frac{\Delta A}{V_{0}}+\cdots\right],

(223)

where
A_{0}
and
\Delta A
are the leading order and the first corrections of the gradient expansion:

A(q)=\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\,q_{\alpha}\mathcal{A}_{+}^{\alpha}(x,k)=A_{0}+\Delta A,

(224)

and similarly for
V
. We notice that, exactly as for the massive case, the first order dissipative corrections to the axial part of the Wigner function require a breaking of parity. Therefore, the dissipative contribution to the massless spin polarization coming from
\Delta A
also requires a breaking of parity. Instead,
\Delta V
can have non chiral dissipative corrections, see eq. (
237
). However, at first order, the dissipative corrections to the massless spin polarization coming from
\Delta V
are proportional to the ratio of
A_{0}
and
V_{0}
, and in order to have a non-vanishing
A_{0}
, a violation of parity symmetry is needed. In conclusion, also for the massless case, first order dissipative corrections are allowed only by breaking parity.

As the spin polarization of a massless fermion with the correct formulas (
73
) and (
75
) has only been studied at global equilibrium
[
85
]
, we compute some contributions for a free Dirac field in presence of chiral imbalance
[
75
,
74
]
. The leading order Wigner function evaluated with the statistical operator (
59
) is

\langle\widehat{W}_{+}(x,k)\rangle_{\beta}=\left(\frac{1+\gamma^{5}}{2}n_{F}^{R}(x,k)+\frac{1-\gamma^{5}}{2}n_{F}^{L}(x,k)\right)\gamma^{\nu}k_{\nu}\frac{\delta(k^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)}{(2\pi)^{3}},

(225)

with the distribution functions given in eq. (
221
),
from which it follows that the leading order axial and vector parts of the Wigner function are

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{+}^{\mu}\rangle_{\beta}=

\displaystyle\frac{4\delta(k^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{n_{F}^{R}(x,k)+n_{F}^{L}(x,k)}{2}k^{\mu},

(226a)

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{+}^{\mu}\rangle_{\beta}=

\displaystyle\frac{4\delta(k^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{n_{F}^{R}(x,k)-n_{F}^{L}(x,k)}{2}k^{\mu}.

(226b)

To write down the expression we need to choose a vector
q
, such that
q^{2}=0
and
q\cdot k\neq 0
. Denoting with
\mathfrak{p}=(\kappa,0,0,\kappa)
the standard form and with
[k]
the standard Lorentz transformation of
k=(|\bm{k}|,\bm{k})
, such that
k^{\mu}=[k]^{\mu}_{\,\nu}\mathfrak{p}^{\nu}
, a convenient choice for
q
is
q^{\mu}=\bar{k}^{\mu}=[k]^{\mu}_{\,\nu}\bar{\mathfrak{p}}^{\nu}
where

\mathfrak{p}=(\kappa,0,0,-\kappa)
. In this way we have the decomposition
k^{\mu}=(k\cdot u)u^{\mu}+k_{\perp}^{\mu}
and
\bar{k}^{\mu}=(k\cdot u)u^{\mu}-k_{\perp}^{\mu}
. With this choice and from eqs. (
226
) and (
223
), we readily obtain

S^{\mu}_{0}(|\bm{k}|,\bm{k})=\frac{k^{\mu}}{2}\frac{A_{0}}{V_{0}}=\frac{k^{\mu}}{2}\frac{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\,\left[n_{F}(\beta\cdot k-\zeta-\zeta_{A})-n_{F}(\beta\cdot k-\zeta+\zeta_{A})\right]}{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\,\left[n_{F}(\beta\cdot k-\zeta-\zeta_{A})+n_{F}(\beta\cdot k-\zeta+\zeta_{A})\right]},

(227)

which vanish in absence of parity breaking, i.e.
\zeta_{A}=0
.
We now consider the first order contribution induced by thermal vorticity; for the axial and vector part of Wigner function of a free field, we found:

\Delta_{\varpi}\mathcal{A}_{+}^{\mu}=-a^{\rm Free}_{\varpi}\frac{2{\widetilde{\varpi}}^{\mu\nu}k_{\nu}}{(2\pi)^{3}},\quad\Delta_{\varpi}\mathcal{V}_{+}^{\mu}=-\mathfrak{v}^{c{\rm Free}}_{\varpi}\frac{2{\widetilde{\varpi}}^{\mu\nu}k_{\nu}}{(2\pi)^{3}},

(228)

where

\displaystyle a^{\rm Free}_{\varpi}=

\displaystyle\delta(k^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)\frac{n_{F}^{R}(\beta\cdot k)\left(1-n_{F}^{R}(\beta\cdot k)\right)+n_{F}^{L}(\beta\cdot k)\left(1-n_{F}^{L}(\beta\cdot k)\right)}{2},

(229)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}^{c{\rm Free}}_{\varpi}=

\displaystyle\delta(k^{2})\theta(k\cdot u)\frac{n_{F}^{R}(\beta\cdot k)\left(1-n_{F}^{R}(\beta\cdot k)\right)-n_{F}^{L}(\beta\cdot k)\left(1-n_{F}^{L}(\beta\cdot k)\right)}{2}.

(230)

Choosing
q=\bar{k}
and defining

H=\frac{\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\bar{k}_{\mu}k_{\nu}\varpi_{\rho\sigma}}{2(k\cdot\bar{k})},

(231)

we obtain

\begin{split}\Delta_{\varpi}S^{\mu}(|\bm{k}|,\,\bm{k})=&\frac{k^{\mu}}{2}\frac{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\,\frac{H}{2}\left(n_{F}^{R}\left(1-n_{F}^{R}\right)+n_{F}^{L}\left(1-n_{F}^{L}\right)\right)}{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\,\left(n_{F}^{R}+n_{F}^{L}\right)}\\
&+\frac{k^{\mu}}{2}\frac{A_{0}}{V_{0}}\frac{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\,\frac{H}{2}\left(n_{F}^{R}\left(1-n_{F}^{R}\right)-n_{F}^{L}\left(1-n_{F}^{L}\right)\right)}{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\,\left(n_{F}^{R}+n_{F}^{L}\right)}.\end{split}

(232)

This expression at global equilibrium with
\zeta_{A}=0
reduces to

\Delta_{\varpi}S^{\mu}(|\bm{k}|,\,\bm{k})=-\frac{k^{\mu}}{2}\frac{H}{2}\left(1-n_{F}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\varpi^{2}\right)

(233)

in accordance with
[
85
]
.

Other contributions can be obtained in a similar fashion. However, one must be careful on how to choose
q
as it might be orthogonal to terms contributing to the axial and vector parts of the Wigner function. For instance the spin Hall effect term contracted with
q=\bar{k}
is vanishing:
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\bar{k}_{\mu}k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}=\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\bar{k}^{\perp}_{\mu}k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}=-\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}k^{\perp}_{\mu}k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}=0
.
In these cases, it is more convenient to choose
q^{\mu}={\widetilde{k}}^{\mu}=[k]^{\mu}_{\,\nu}{\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\nu}
with
{\widetilde{\mathfrak{p}}}=(\kappa,\kappa,0,0)
, which has the decomposition
{\widetilde{k}}^{\mu}=(k\cdot u)u^{\mu}+{\widetilde{k}}_{\perp}^{\mu}
, with
{\widetilde{k}}_{\perp}\cdot k_{\perp}=0
. With this choice and with the results of previous section we obtain:

\begin{split}\Delta_{\rm SHE}S^{\mu}&(|\bm{k}|,\,\bm{k})=-\frac{k^{\mu}}{4}\epsilon^{\alpha\nu\rho\sigma}{\widetilde{k}}_{\alpha}k_{\nu}\\
&\times\frac{\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k}{(k\cdot u)}\,u_{\sigma}\left[n_{F}^{R}\left(1-n_{F}^{R}\right)\left(1-\frac{A_{0}}{V_{0}}\right)+n_{F}^{L}\left(1-n_{F}^{L}\right)\left(1+\frac{A_{0}}{V_{0}}\right)\right]\partial_{\rho}\zeta}{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\,({\widetilde{k}}\cdot k)\left(n_{F}^{R}+n_{F}^{L}\right)}\\
&-\frac{k^{\mu}}{4}\epsilon^{\alpha\nu\rho\sigma}{\widetilde{k}}_{\alpha}k_{\nu}\frac{\int_{\Sigma}\frac{{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k}{(k\cdot u)}\,u_{\sigma}\left[n_{F}^{R}\left(1-n_{F}^{R}\right)\left(1-\frac{A_{0}}{V_{0}}\right)-n_{F}^{L}\left(1-n_{F}^{L}\right)\left(1+\frac{A_{0}}{V_{0}}\right)\right]\partial_{\rho}\zeta_{A}}{\int_{\Sigma}{\rm d}\Sigma\cdot k\,({\widetilde{k}}\cdot k)\left(n_{F}^{R}+n_{F}^{L}\right)}.\end{split}

(234)

5.4
The vector part of Wigner function up to first order in hydrodynamic gradients

The results for the vector part of the Wigner function are also reported.
We found that in general there are 15 non-dissipative and non-chiral possible contributions to the vector Wigner function:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm LTE}\mathcal{V}_{+}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle v_{r_{A}\epsilon}^{c}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\partial_{\langle\rho\rangle}\zeta_{A}+\left[v^{c}_{fu}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+v^{c}_{f\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+v^{c}_{fk}Q^{\mu\rho}\right]f_{\rho}

(235a)

\displaystyle+v^{c}_{\Upsilon\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\Upsilon_{\rho}+\left[v^{c}_{Iu}u^{\mu}+v^{c}_{Ik}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]I+v^{c}_{I-\Upsilon\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}(I_{\rho}-\Upsilon_{\rho})

(235b)

\displaystyle+\left[v^{c}_{I_{S}u}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}}{k_{\perp}^{2}}u^{\mu}+v^{c}_{I_{S}\Delta}\frac{\Delta^{\mu\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}+v^{c}_{I_{S}k}\frac{Q^{\mu\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]I_{S\,\rho\sigma}

(235c)

\displaystyle+\left[v^{c}_{\rm{S12}\Delta}\Delta^{\tau\rho}\Delta^{\mu\sigma}+v^{c}_{\rm{S12}k}Q^{\tau\rho}\Delta^{\mu\sigma}\right]\Phi^{S12}_{\tau,\rho\sigma}

(235d)

\displaystyle+\left[v^{c}_{\rm{S13}\Delta}\Delta^{\tau\sigma}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+v^{c}_{\rm{S13}k}Q^{\tau\sigma}\Delta^{\mu\rho}\right]\Phi^{S13}_{\tau,\rho\sigma},

(235e)

and 13 non-dissipative and chiral coefficients

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\rm LTE},\chi}\mathcal{V}_{+}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle\left[\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{\zeta_{A}u}u^{\mu}+\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{\zeta_{A}k}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]\zeta_{A}+\mathfrak{v}_{r\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\partial_{\langle\rho\rangle}\zeta

(236a)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{\varpi}\frac{2{\widetilde{\varpi}}^{\mu\nu}k_{\nu}}{(2\pi)^{3}}-(\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{wu}-\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{w\Delta})\frac{(k\cdot w)}{(k\cdot u)}u^{\mu}

(236b)

\displaystyle+(\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{\alpha\epsilon}-\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{w\Delta})\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}\alpha_{\rho}+\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{wk}Q^{\mu\rho}w_{\rho}

(236c)

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{\mathfrak{S}-\varpi}\frac{2({\widetilde{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\mu\nu}-{\widetilde{\varpi}}^{\mu\nu})k_{\nu}}{(2\pi)^{3}}-(\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{\mathfrak{w}-wu}-\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{\mathfrak{w}-w\Delta})\frac{k^{\rho}_{\perp}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}(\mathfrak{w}-w)_{\rho}

(236d)

\displaystyle+(\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{\mathfrak{a}-\alpha\epsilon}-\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{\mathfrak{w}-w\Delta})\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}(\mathfrak{a}-\alpha)_{\rho}+\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{\mathfrak{w}-wk}Q^{\mu\rho}(\mathfrak{w}-w)_{\rho}

(236e)

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{q\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}(\beta Du_{\rho}+\partial_{\rho}\beta)+\mathfrak{v}^{c}_{\sigma\epsilon}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}\frac{u_{\nu}k_{\alpha}}{(k\cdot u)}\beta\sigma_{\rho\sigma}.

(236f)

Regarding dissipative contributions, we found 19 non-chiral possible contributions

\displaystyle\Delta_{\rm D}\mathcal{V}_{+}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle\left[\bar{v}_{D\zeta u}u^{\mu}+\bar{v}_{D\zeta k}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]D\zeta+\left[\bar{v}_{ru}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\bar{v}_{r\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+\bar{v}_{rk}Q^{\mu\rho}\right]\partial_{\langle\rho\rangle}\zeta

(237a)

\displaystyle+\bar{v}^{c}_{\mathfrak{a}-\alpha\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}(\mathfrak{a}-\alpha)_{\rho}-\bar{v}^{c}_{\mathfrak{a}-\alpha u}\frac{k^{\rho}_{\perp}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}(\mathfrak{a}-\alpha)_{\rho}+\bar{v}^{c}_{\mathfrak{w}-w\epsilon}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}(\mathfrak{w}-w)_{\rho}

(237b)

\displaystyle+\bar{v}^{c}_{\mathfrak{a}-\alpha k}Q^{\mu\rho}(\mathfrak{a}-\alpha)_{\rho}+\left[\bar{v}^{c}_{D\beta u}u^{\mu}+\bar{v}^{c}_{D\beta k}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]D\beta+\left[\bar{v}^{c}_{\theta u}u^{\mu}+\bar{v}^{c}_{\theta k}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]\beta\theta

(237c)

\displaystyle+\bar{v}^{c}_{\sigma u}(k\cdot u)\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}}{k_{\perp}^{2}}u^{\mu}\,\beta\sigma_{\rho\sigma}+\bar{v}^{c}_{\sigma\delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}\beta\sigma_{\rho\sigma}+\bar{v}^{c}_{\sigma k}Q^{\mu\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}\,\beta\sigma_{\rho\sigma}

(237d)

\displaystyle+\left[\bar{v}^{c}_{qu}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\bar{v}^{c}_{q\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+\bar{v}^{c}_{qk}Q^{\mu\rho}\right](\beta Du_{\rho}+\partial_{\rho}\beta),

(237e)

and 19 possible chiral contributions

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\rm D},\chi}\mathcal{V}_{+}^{\mu}=

\displaystyle\left[\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{D\zeta_{A}u}u^{\mu}+\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{D\zeta_{A}k}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]D\zeta_{A}+\left[\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{r_{A}u}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{r_{A}\Delta}^{c}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{r_{A}k}^{c}Q^{\mu\rho}\right]\partial_{\langle\rho\rangle}\zeta_{A}

(238a)

\displaystyle+\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{f\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k^{\perp}_{\nu}u_{\sigma}}{(k\cdot u)}f_{\rho}+\left[\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{\Upsilon u}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{\Upsilon\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{\Upsilon k}Q^{\mu\rho}\right]\Upsilon_{\rho}

(238b)

\displaystyle+\left[\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{I-\Upsilon u}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\rho}u^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{I-\Upsilon\Delta}\Delta^{\mu\rho}+\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{I-\Upsilon k}Q^{\mu\rho}\right](I_{\rho}-\Upsilon_{\rho})+\left[\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{\phi u}u^{\mu}+\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{\phi k}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\mu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]\phi

(238c)

\displaystyle+\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{I_{S}\epsilon}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\rho}k_{\perp}^{\sigma}\frac{u_{\nu}k^{\perp}_{\alpha}}{(k\cdot u)^{2}}I_{S\,\rho\sigma}+\left[\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{\rm{S12}\Delta\epsilon}\Delta^{\mu\tau}\epsilon^{\lambda\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{u_{\lambda}k^{\perp}_{\nu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{\rm{S12}\epsilon}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\tau}u_{\nu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]\Phi^{S12}_{\tau,\rho\sigma}

(238d)

\displaystyle+\left[\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{\rm{S13}\Delta\epsilon}\Delta^{\mu\tau}\epsilon^{\lambda\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{u_{\lambda}k^{\perp}_{\nu}}{(k\cdot u)}+\bar{\mathfrak{v}}^{c}_{\rm{S13}\epsilon}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\frac{k_{\perp}^{\tau}u_{\nu}}{(k\cdot u)}\right]\Phi^{S13}_{\tau,\rho\sigma}.

(238e)

The same quantities leading to non-dissipative (dissipative) contributions to the axial part also give non-dissipative (dissipative) contributions to the axial part but with opposite chirality. For this reason, the gradients of the spin potential can give a local thermal equilibrium, hence non-dissipative, contribution to the vector part.

6
Summary and outlook

In summary, the first-order gradient expansion of
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\mu}
, that is the axial and vector parts of the Wigner function of a fermion, has been obtained, including the dissipative effects. These quantities are related to the spin polarization of a massive or massless fermion in a fluid trough eq. (
72
) and eq. (
73
) respectively. The main results are given in eqs. (
207
)-(
210
) for the axial part and in eqs. (
235
)-(
238
) for the vector part. The definitions and decompositions of the hydrodynamic fields used to write down all the contributions are given in eqs. (
129
)-(
137
). All the contributions are proportional to coefficients that are functions of the momentum
k
and of the point
x
where the Wigner function is evaluated. The values of these coefficients depend on the microscopic theory considered and can be evaluated with the momentum dependent Kubo formulas listed in section
4
. All of these effects and their coefficients have been classified as either non-dissipative or dissipative, and either chiral or non-chiral. Dissipative coefficients are those induced by the dissipative part of the statistical operator (i.e., the part responsible for the increase of the entropy of the system), while the non-dissipative comes from the local thermal equilibrium part, which does not increase entropy. Chiral coefficients are those that are not vanishing only if the equilibrium statistical operator used to evaluate them is not symmetric under parity transformation, for instance if it has a chiral imbalance or it contains interactions that break parity.

We found that up to first order, all the dissipative corrections to the massive and massless spin polarization are chiral contributions, with the only exception of the spin polarization induced by the gradient of the spin potential. From this observation we can expect that the chiral dissipative effects can be neglected for the spin polarization in heavy-ion collisions. Instead, if the spin degrees of freedom are not equilibrated, the gradient of spin potential can have a seizable impact on the data. We leave the calculation of these dissipative coefficients and their phenomenological relevance to future works.

For the non-dissipative coefficients, we found that in addition to the usual spin polarization induced by vorticity, there could be other independent contributions to spin polarization induced by vorticity if interactions break the degeneracy of some coefficients that cancel out for free fields. This situation is similar to the spin alignment induced by the thermal shear. It is allowed by symmetry, but it turns out to be vanishing for a free field
[
83
]
. However, it could be non-vanishing because of interactions
[
81
,
101
,
102
,
103
]
or of finite volume effects. Moreover, we found that in addition to the spin Hall effect, where spin polarization is induce by the gradient of
\mu/T
, there are other chiral effects that generate polarization and a vector part of the Wigner function with the gradients of
\mu/T
and
\mu_{A}/T
.

The insights gained from this work have direct phenomenological implications for understanding spin phenomena in various collision systems.
One critical application lies in the study of proton-lead (p-Pb) collisions. Unlike nucleus-nucleus collisions, p-Pb collisions are characterized by a medium that is inherently more out-of-equilibrium. The dissipative effects meticulously derived in this paper are then expected to be more prominent and relevant in p-Pb collisions. The Kubo formulas provided offer crucial theoretical predictions for measurable spin phenomena in these systems, potentially explaining discrepancies observed in current experimental data
[
104
,
105
]
.

Furthermore, the findings presented in this paper are highly relevant to understanding spin phenomena at lower collision energies. It has been observed from ideal spin hydrodynamics that at lower energies the relaxation time for spin is larger
[
33
]
. This indicates that the spin degrees of freedom may not fully equilibrate, necessitating the inclusion of the spin potential and its gradients in theoretical descriptions. Our formulas provide a framework for more accurate spin polarization predictions beyond the local thermal equilibrium descriptions at these energies.

Finally, the chiral spin Hall effect could be used to probe the topological configurations of Quantum Chromodynamics (QCD). This effect, where local spin polarization is induced by gradients of temperature and axial chemical potential in a direction orthogonal to both the particle’s momentum and the gradients, offers a novel observable. Its phenomenological application could involve utilizing this effect to study the anisotropies of the topological configurations of QCD. In the presence of a chiral imbalance, as might arise from sphaleron transitions in the early stages of heavy-ion collisions, the chiral spin Hall effect provides a direct link between macroscopic spin observables and the underlying topological charge fluctuations. Detecting and characterizing this effect experimentally could provide invaluable information about the vacuum structure of QCD, and elucidate the dynamics of chiral anomaly in hot and dense nuclear matter. This opens up exciting new avenues for exploring the interplay between hydrodynamics, spin, and the topological sectors of QCD.
